At Zhangjiabao, 1.5 km northwest of Qianxian County town, there are two 8-m-high earthen mounds, which are the remains of the first gate passage of Qianling; 3 km north are the South Two Peaks; from the two peaks northward is the spirit path, with stone carvings on both sides including a pair of stone huabiao (ornamental columns, which in ancient times symbolized the emperor's willingness to receive remonstrance), a pair of flying horses, a pair of vermilion birds, five pairs of stone horses (including three pairs of horse handlers), and ten pairs of stone figures holding swords with both hands; 13.5 m north of the stone figures are the Shushengji Stele (in the west) and the Wordless Stele (in the east); behind them are 61 stone figures, which are statues of foreign tribal chief envoys who attended the funeral; the Vermilion Bird Gate is at the southern foot of Liangshan Mountain, with a pair of stone lions in front of the gate, and north of the stone lions is the site of the Xiandian (offering hall).

The stone carving group system of Qianling had some influence on other later imperial Mausoleums; for example, Emperor Suzong's Jianling and Emperor Ruizong's Qiaoling both followed the Qianling system.

The Qianling Mausoleum park is built using natural mountain peaks. The north peak of Liangshan Mountain is where the Qianling underground palace is located.

Tall city walls built around the north peak are called the palace city, with a gate opened on each side, and stone carvings and gate towers set outside the gates.

Outside the Vermilion Bird Gate, a broad spirit path is built using the low-lying area, with its southern end reaching directly to the foot of the Milk Peaks.

Between the two Milk Peaks, a natural gateway to the Mausoleum park is formed, with gate towers built on top of the Milk Peaks.

Together with the stone carving artworks such as huabiao, winged horses, vermilion birds, and stone figures on both sides of the spirit path, they jointly create the pre-Mausoleum architectural environment, forming a strong spatial contrast with the natural mountain peak above the underground palace—the North Peak.

The tomb passage and tomb gate are located at the middle waist of the southern slope of the mountain, chiseled into the natural limestone mountain. The tomb passage is a north-south slope, with a total length of about 65 m and an east-west width of 3.87 m.

Qianling originally had inner and outer double cities, and the inner city wall foundation remains still exist.

Its south city wall foundation is 1,438 m long; 657 m from east to west, there is a rammed-earth mound remnant 1 m above ground and 10 m in diameter, which is the site of the Vermilion Bird Gate.

The south city wall has water drainage culverts built where it crosses the Huangchao Gully, constructed with stone strips.

The north city wall foundation is 1,450 m long, the east city wall foundation is 1,582 m long, and the west city wall foundation is 1,450 m long; the city wall foundation rammed earth is 2.1 to 2.5 m wide.

City corner remains: the southeast city corner is 7.5 m high, the northeast city corner is 5.1 m high, the northwest city corner is 5.5 m high, and the southwest city corner is 10 m high, all built of rammed earth.

The northwest and northeast city corners have stone strip-built foundations. Within the Qianling Mausoleum area, 17 companion tombs are distributed.

Among them, Crown Prince Yide Li Chongrun's tomb was designated as a 'Mausoleum' (hao mu wei ling), making it one of the largest and highest-ranking Tang imperial Mausoleum companion tombs ever excavated.

Princess Yongtai Li Xianhui's tomb was also designated as a 'Mausoleum,' making her the only one among 212 Tang princesses to receive this规格 treatment, and also the earliest excavated and relatively high-ranking Tang female imperial tomb.

When Crown Prince Zhanghuai Li Xian was reburied, he was accompanied with the rites of Prince Yong, the lowest rank. Emperor Xuanzong's Tailing is located at Jinsu Mountain, 15 km northeast of Pucheng County town.

It was designated as the fifth batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 2001. Jinsu Mountain is 4.2 km east-west and 2 km north-south, divided into two larger peaks, east and west.

The Tailing Mausoleum park surrounds the west peak, 1.2 km east-west and 1.3 km north-south, with a roughly square plane; the tomb chamber is located on the north-south gate axis, and the east and west gates are respectively set on the ridges extending left and right from the top of the west peak.

Tailing uses the mountain as the Mausoleum, chiseling the tomb chamber into the mountain belly, and building Mausoleum walls around the foot of the mountain, divided into inner and outer cities.

The inner city wall originally had four gates on all four sides; limited by terrain, the four gate sites were chosen according to local conditions, not in a symmetrical layout.

Except for the south gate located on the gentle slope in front of the mountain, the east, west, and north gates are all built on ridges or hillside platforms, with narrow areas.

The straight-line distance between the east and west gates is 1,168.8 m, and between the south and north gates is 1,133.8 m.

Each of the four corners of the city wall has a corner gate tower, with great disparities in distances between corner gate towers; for example, the straight-line distance from the southwest corner gate tower to the southeast corner gate tower is 1,176.9 m, while the straight-line distance from the northwest corner gate tower to the northeast corner gate tower is only 765.3 m.

Affected by terrain, the city wall plane is roughly an irregular pentagon. Except for the south wall being relatively straight, the other three walls are not straight extensions.

The north wall starts from the northwest corner gate tower, bends northeast to connect with the north gate, then connects with the closer northeast corner gate tower; the west wall is built on the slope on the west side of the mountain ridge, roughly arc-shaped; the east wall basically winds and undulates along the ridge direction, in a snake-like shape.

Tailing's above-ground buildings include the Xiandian (offering hall), lower palace, and Mausoleum office, which no longer exist today.

Its companion tomb, according to records in 'Tang Huiyao,' 'Chang'an Zhi,' 'Wenxian Tongkao,' and 'Guanzhong Lingmu Zhi,' is only one, namely the already excavated Gao Lishi tomb.

Tailing's stone carvings are relatively well preserved in quantity, but in poor condition.

Tailing's stone carvings (excluding foreign chief statues) should originally have 50 pieces (groups) (war horses, bases, and horse handlers counted as one group), with 41 pieces (groups) currently existing on the ground.

9 pieces (groups) of stone carvings were newly cleaned and unearthed, including the fourth war horse base on the west side of the south gate spirit path, 4 fragments of foreign chief statues, 1 foreign chief statue base, the third war horse on the east side of the north gate spirit path (including war horse and base, horse handler) and the first war horse on the west side (including war horse and base), and a stone tiger and a stone figure blank were also unearthed at the north gate.

Emperor Xuanzong's Zhenling is located at Beizhong Mountain, north of Cuihuang Village, Baiwang Township, Jingyang County. It was designated as the fifth batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 2001.

The Zhenling Mausoleum park spans Jingyang and Chunhua counties north-south.

The terrain within the park is complex, with steep mountains; on the surface, only the Quetai (magpie platform) sites, corner gate tower sites, earthen gate towers in front of each gate, and above-ground stone carvings remain.

The Mausoleum park city wall surrounds Beizhong Mountain, built according to the natural mountain terrain.

Its layout is an irregular shape narrow in the south and wide in the north; except for the south gate located at the relatively gentle southern foot of Beizhong Mountain, the other three gates are all built on ridges.

The city foundation is rammed; the south wall is located in the relatively gentle area at the foot of the mountain, extending in a straight line; the west wall winds northward from the southwest corner gate tower along the ridge to the west gate, then connects northwestward along the ridge to the northwest corner gate tower; the east wall has a gentler slope northward from the southeast corner gate tower, taking a straight line to the east gate, and north of the east gate winds along the ridge to the northeast corner gate tower; the north wall has the most complex terrain, with the western part明显 extending toward the southwest ridge, and connections to both the northeast and northwest corner gate towers requiring passing through steep slopes and gullies, with large undulations.

The Zhenling lower palace site is located on the platform north of Miaobeihou Village, Baiwang Town, Jingyang County, 299.24 m southeast of the west Quetai site, with terrain high in the north and low in the south.

The palace city overall plane is rectangular, oriented 8 degrees west of north, 248.39 m north-south and 172.11 m east-west, with a gate only in the center of the south side; the building remains are relatively severely damaged.

There are two larger rammed-earth foundation sites, one inside the south gate of the palace city and one in the center of the palace city; both building foundation sites and the south gate are on the central axis of the palace city.

The Zhenling spirit path stone carving group from south to north依次 includes: 1 pair of stone pillars, 1 pair of winged horses, 1 pair of ostriches (only the east piece现存), 5 pairs each of war horses and horse handlers (originally 10 each, currently 6 war horses existing, 2 horse handlers newly discovered), and 10 pairs of stone figures (originally 20, currently 11 existing on the ground, 4 newly discovered).

At the foreign chief hall building site south of the south gate tower, 6 foreign chief stone figure statues were unearthed.

The Zhenling north gate stone carvings, besides 1 pair of stone lions, should have 3 pairs of war horses and horse handlers, with 4 war horses currently existing on the ground and 1 horse handler newly discovered.

Compared with the Qianling stone carving group, its stone carving group is relatively roughly carved.

关键特征 / Key Features

  • 唐朝自高祖李渊公元618年称帝到唐哀帝李柷公元907年禅位
  • 共历高祖、太宗、高宗、玄宗等21帝289年
  • 因武则天与唐高宗合葬
  • 故唐王陵共计20座
  • 除昭宗和陵、哀帝温陵两座陵墓外
  • 其余18座均位于陕西关中地区
  • 其东起蒲城、富平、三原、泾阳、礼泉
  • 西止乾县
  • 东西横亘150公里
  • 人称关中十八陵
  • 唐陵基本形制可分为两类
  • 一种是建于高塬的覆斗形土冢
  • 包括营建于三原县荆塬和徐木塬之上的献、庄、端三陵
  • 以及建于乾县北塬之上的靖陵
  • 其所在地海拔皆在500至800米以上

第八章 佛塔、石窟

Chapter 8 Buddhist Pagodas and Grottoes

90. 唐代佛塔（大雁塔、小雁塔、玄奘塔、善导塔）

Tang Dynasty Pagodas (Giant Wild Goose Pagoda, Small Wild Goose Pagoda, Xuanzang Pagoda, Shandao Pagoda)
唐 佛塔建筑 全国重点文物保护单位 西安市

陕西是全国保留古塔较多的省份之一。截至2006年年底，陕西境内现存不同时期的古塔共287座。从时间与空间分布来看，陕西唐代古塔主要集中在京城长安附近；宋塔则主要集中在通往北部、西北部的军事要道上；

明代佛塔主要集中于陕西北部；清代分布地区稍显平均。从建筑材料来看，砖塔最多，为164座，占全部古塔的57.1%；

其余类型分别有：石塔102座、砖石塔4座、铁塔5座、土塔10座、木塔1座、琉璃塔1座。

陕西古塔种类齐全，造型多样，有楼阁式、密檐式、喇嘛式、单层式。唐宋古塔中，楼阁式塔是主体，西安大雁塔、玄奘塔皆属此类。

楼阁式塔从结构上又可细分为空心楼阁式和实心楼阁式两种。陕西密檐式塔除一层高大外，其上各层的高度逐步递减，下面几层都还有门窗等装饰，只是越往上随着层间距离的不断缩短，才出现密集的塔檐。

如陕西富县的福严院塔，最后几层突然加大收分，缩短层间距离，使整体塔形犹如曲线柔和的纺锤。陕西造像塔以明代为主，现存最早的造像塔是保存在耀州区药王山上的三座北魏时的小型造像塔。

明代造像塔种类繁多，如咸阳千佛铁塔、延安琉璃塔、志丹县盘龙禅院石塔、赵石畔石塔等造像塔。

陕西经幢式塔主要分布在陕北和陕南两个地区，前者如明代前期的绥德县的宝台寺塔和兴善寺普同塔，后者如旬阳县圆通寺的两座明代舍利塔，两塔均为须弥座，单层六边形塔身，八边形宝盖式塔顶。

现存陕西喇嘛式塔多为明代所修，横山县的清凉寺喇嘛塔和位于武功县的释迦文佛舍利宝塔，其特征是高大的须弥座、覆钵式塔身、粗壮的十三相轮、华盖、塔刹。

大雁塔又名大慈恩寺塔，位于今西安城南大慈恩寺内。塔造型简洁，气势雄伟，是我国佛教建筑艺术中不可多得的杰作，也是唐长安城保留至今的重要标志之一。

1961年公布为第一批全国重点文物保护单位。大雁塔建于唐永徽三年（公元652年），是唐高宗李治为安置玄奘由印度带回的佛教经籍而专门建造。

大雁塔初建时方形五层，砖表土心，玄奘法师亲自参加建塔，搬运砖石，历时两年才建成。但因该塔为砖表土心，50多年后逐渐崩坏。唐长安年间（公元701至704年），用青砖改建为七层方形楼阁式空心砖塔。

唐大历年间（公元778至779年），又改建成十层。后经战火破坏，剩下七层。

五代长兴年间（公元930至933年）、明成化二年（1466年）、明万历三十二年（公元1604年）皆以武则天时重建的式样为本进行维修。

特别是明万历年间的维修，对残破的塔身加砌面砖，将唐塔整体包住，塔内部重修塔梯，形成了今日大雁塔的基本形态。现存大雁塔为砖木结构的四方形楼阁式塔，由塔基、塔身、塔刹三部分组成。

全塔通高64.5米，塔基高4.2米，南北长约48.7米，东西长约45.7米；塔身底层边长25.5米，呈方锥形；塔刹高4.87米。

塔内平面亦呈方形，各层均有木楼板，设置木扶梯，可盘旋而上至塔顶。

各楼层厚度为：首层10.36米，二层7.37米，三层7.15米，四层6.65米，五层6.7米，六层6.4米，七层5.2米（塔顶厚度不计）。

大雁塔塔身仿木结构。明代包砖后，立面用倚柱分隔成仿木构楼阁式样，首层和二层每面为九间，三、四层为七间，五、六、七层为五间。首层正面当心间开券门，以上每层、每面的当心间有券窗。

塔身各层壁面都用砖砌扁柱和阑额，柱的上部施有大斗，柱间砌砖隐出阑额、柱头栌斗。每层以叠涩出檐划分，檐下砌出菱角牙子。塔顶叠置宝瓶、葫芦作为全塔的结束。

塔体南侧券门内两侧砖龛嵌有《大唐三藏圣教序》和《大唐三藏圣教序记》两通石碑，西侧石门楣刻有唐代著名书法家褚遂良所书写。两侧券门楣石镌刻有唐刻建筑图案和佛像等线刻画，画面布局严谨，线条遒劲流畅，内容细致入微，传说出自唐代著名画家阎立本和尉迟乙僧之手，是研究中国唐代建筑形制的重要资料。

小雁塔又名荐福寺塔，位于今西安城南荐福寺内。塔造型挺秀柔和，风格简洁古朴，为唐代密檐式塔的代表作品。1961年公布为第一批全国重点文物保护单位。

荐福寺原名献福寺，始建于唐睿宗文明元年（公元684年），天授元年（公元690年）改名荐福寺。

唐中宗景龙年间（公元707至709年），于荐福寺南面的安仁坊内另辟塔院，建造了一座砖塔，作为荐福寺塔。此塔在唐时称雁塔，明时也有称荐福塔，清时才出现小雁塔之称。唐末战乱，荐福寺屡遭破坏，寺院毁废，只有小雁塔得以保存。

据宋元祐年间（公元1086至1094年）文献记载，彼时荐福寺已从最初的开化坊迁入安仁坊的塔院内，与小雁塔成为整体。

宋徽宗政和六年（1116年），将风化严重的塔檐、塔角修好，以白土粉饰，至今塔身可见白土粉刷的痕迹。明、清两朝对荐福寺和小雁塔进行过多次修缮，明代曾有五次大规模的整修，基本上保留先有的格局。

小雁塔原为方形十五层密檐楼阁式空心青砖塔。明宪宗成化二十三年（1487年）地震，塔自顶而下中裂；明嘉靖三十四年（1556年）地震，塔顶震塌两层，现残存13层。

1989年测定，总塔高43.395米，底边长为11.38米，高与底边的比例约为100比26。

小雁塔由基座和塔身两部分构成，现存基座是砖砌的方形台基，基座下有竖穴式的地宫。基座为砖方台，基座南北各开有一券门，青石门楣，券门下为青石踏步。石门框上布满精美的唐代线刻，尤其门楣上的天人供养图像，艺术价值很高。底层北券门外紧靠塔体的砖砌门楼，系清代增建。

塔基座南侧有清代石门坊，南额刻有万汇沾恩，北额刻有不二法门。基座之上为塔身，塔身首层较高，二层以上高度逐层递减，塔体自下而上略呈弧线形，庄严中不乏秀丽。小雁塔第一层塔身南北开辟门洞，以供出入，南北门框均以青石做成，石质门楣上线刻供养天人和蔓草、祥云、迦陵频伽等具典型佛教寓意的装饰图案，反映了初唐时期的艺术风格。

塔身结构为单壁中空，内设有木构式的楼层，有木梯盘旋而上可达塔顶。塔壁不设柱额，每层砖砌出檐，檐部叠涩砖，间以二层菱角牙子，以加强檐口线。塔的第一层外墙明代做过包砌。塔上塔窗左右用叠涩砖砌成低矮的平座，与一般密檐式塔形制不同。

塔身宽度由下至上逐层递减，愈上愈促，形成自然的收分，呈现出秀丽舒畅的卷杀轮廓。塔各层南北有半圆形拱门。

兴教寺玄奘塔位于西安市长安区杜曲镇西韦村兴教寺内的西慈恩塔院内，是我国现存最早的楼阁型方形砖塔，亦是现存最早的一座唐代仿木结构楼阁式砖塔。

1961年公布为第一批全国重点文物保护单位。唐高宗麟德元年（公元664年）玄奘法师圆寂后，初葬于白鹿塬。

唐总章二年（公元669年）迁葬于此，并修寺建塔，以资纪念。寺建成以后，屡遭兵火，各代也屡有修葺。19世纪后期、清同治年间，寺内殿舍皆为兵火所焚，唯三座灵塔幸存。塔体为方形五层楼阁式实心砖塔，通高约21米，底层边长5.2米。

底层南面辟龛室，供奉泥塑玄奘法师像。北壁嵌唐开成四年（公元839年）大唐三藏大遍觉法师塔铭碣一方，因此亦称大遍觉塔。因是墓塔，所以除底层外，其上四层概不能登临。第一层塔身经过后代修缮，已是平素的砖墙，仿木构件无存。

二层以上塔壁作仿木结构，每面三间，以砖隐出倚柱、阑额及普拍枋，斗栱为一斗三升出耍头，无补间铺作。塔顶平砖攒尖，置宝瓶式塔刹（1920、1930年代维修时所加）。

塔身层间以十一层平砖叠涩出檐，其中一、三层为菱角牙子，挑出的檐砖逐层加大，使叠涩呈现出向内曲的弧形曲线，为唐代叠涩塔檐的艺术风格，檐角缀风铃。此塔在结构上除仿木构式样外，还在塔内转角处的柱头上采用半朵铺作（门拱），这种做法在同期的作品中，仅有日本奈良时代的法隆寺西院步廊铺作一例。

自唐之后建塔则多数运用宋、辽砖筑墓室，故塔上用此种门拱制度的实在不多，是我国建筑史上这种做法现存最早的一例。玄奘塔立于兴教寺塔院正中，坐北朝南。其左右两侧各有一座三层塔，为其弟子窥基大师窥基和西明大师圆测舍利塔。

窥基塔于永淳元年（公元682年）始建，大和三年（公元829年）重建。此塔为三级方形楼阁式砖塔，高约7米，底边长2米。

圆测塔于北宋政和五年（1115年）由终南山丰德迁来灵骨时修建，此塔形制与窥基塔基本相同。两塔皆于1961年公布为第一批全国重点文物保护单位。

香积寺塔位于西安市长安区终南山麓神禾塬上的香积寺内，系净土宗门徒为纪念善导和尚所建，故又名善导塔。2001年公布为第五批全国重点文物保护单位。

香积寺、塔建于唐神龙二年（公元706年）。宋兴国三年（公元978年）改名为开和寺，不久又复原名。清乾隆年间对寺、塔进行了局部维修。

善导塔位于香积寺内西北角，是寺内现存最古老的塔。佛塔原为十三级密檐式仿木结构空心砖塔，因唐末地震，塔顶坠落，现仅存11层。该塔平面呈正方形，砖砌单壁结构，内置木梯通至顶层，塔刹为宝瓶葫芦顶。

底层较高，二层骤然变低矮，并逐层收减高宽，塔壁略有收分，至第十层每边长5.1米。经实测塔身至塔顶残高28.95米，包括基座总高31.00米。底层四面有门，南门楣上有乾隆年间所刻涅槃盛事的题字。塔身内四面镌刻楷书金刚经，字体秀雅，笔力遒劲。

香积寺塔的基本形制为密檐式塔，但又具有楼阁式的特征，塔身各层界面均有叠涩砖檐，且沿高度分布密集，显示了密檐式砖塔的特点。

塔身底层平素无饰，以上各层墙面均有突出墙面的仿木构造，每面有4根方形倚柱，将墙面划分为面阔三间，柱上施阑额一道，柱头有栌斗，斗上有间有二道棱角牙子的叠涩出檐，各层当心间设券形龛，梢间有砖砌柱，中为朱绘直棂窗。这些装饰处理又显示了模拟木建筑楼阁的某些特征。

— ENGLISH —

Shaanxi is one of the provinces with more ancient pagodas preserved in China. By the end of 2006, there were 287 ancient pagodas of different periods existing in Shaanxi.

From the temporal and spatial distribution, Tang dynasty ancient pagodas in Shaanxi are mainly concentrated near the capital Chang'an; Song pagodas are mainly concentrated on the military roads leading to the north and northwest; Ming dynasty Buddhist pagodas are mainly concentrated in northern Shaanxi; Qing dynasty distribution is slightly more average by region.

From the perspective of building materials, brick pagodas are the most, at 164, accounting for 57.1% of all ancient pagodas; the other types are: 102 stone pagodas, 4 brick-stone pagodas, 5 iron pagodas, 10 earthen pagodas, 1 wooden pagoda, and 1 glazed pagoda.

Shaanxi ancient pagodas have complete types and diverse forms, including multi-story (ge-style), multi-eave (miyan-style), Lama-style, and single-story.

Among Tang and Song ancient pagodas, multi-story pagodas are the main type, with the Xi'an Giant Wild Goose Pagoda and Xuanzang Pagoda both belonging to this category.

multi-story pagodas can be further subdivided into hollow multi-story and solid multi-story types by structure.

Shaanxi multi-eave pagodas, except for the tall first story, have the heights of each subsequent story gradually decreasing; the lower several stories still have decorations such as doors and windows, and only as the inter-story distance continuously shortens upward do dense eaves appear.

For example, the Fuyan Courtyard Pagoda in Fuxian County, Shaanxi, suddenly increases the收分 in the last several stories, shortening the inter-story distance, making the overall pagoda shape like a softly curved spindle.

Shaanxi statue pagodas are mainly from the Ming dynasty; the earliest existing statue pagodas are three small Northern Wei statue pagodas preserved on Medicine King Mountain in Yaozhou District.

Ming dynasty statue pagodas have many types, such as the Xianyang Thousand-Buddha Iron Pagoda, Yan'an glazed Pagoda, Panlong Chan Courtyard Stone Pagoda in Zhidan County, and Zhaoshipan Stone Pagoda.

Shaanxi jingchuang-style (pillar-style) pagodas are mainly distributed in two regions: northern Shaanxi and southern Shaanxi; the former includes the early Ming Baotai Temple Pagoda and Xingshan Temple Putong Pagoda in Suide County, while the latter includes two Ming dynasty relic pagodas at Yuantong Temple in Xunyang County, both with xumizuo (Sumeru pedestal), single-story hexagonal pagoda bodies, and octagonal baogai (treasure canopy) style pagoda tops.

Most existing Shaanxi Lama-style pagodas were built in the Ming dynasty, such as the Qingliang Temple Lama Pagoda in Hengshan County and the Sakyamuni Buddha relic Pagoda in Wugong County, characterized by tall xumizuo, overturned-bowl pagoda bodies, sturdy thirteen-phase wheels, canopies, and pagoda finials.

The Giant Wild Goose Pagoda, also called the Great Ci'en Temple Pagoda, is located in the Great Ci'en Temple south of present-day Xi'an.

The pagoda has a simple form and majestic momentum, making it a rare masterpiece in Chinese Buddhist architectural art and one of the important landmarks preserved from Tang Chang'an to the present.

It was designated as one of the first batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 1961.

The Giant Wild Goose Pagoda was built in the 3rd year of Yonghui, Tang (652 AD), specially constructed by Emperor Gaozong Li Zhi to house the Buddhist scriptures brought back by Xuanzang from India.

The Giant Wild Goose Pagoda was initially built as a five-story square pagoda with brick exterior and earthen core; Master Xuanzang personally participated in the pagoda construction, carrying bricks and stones, and it took two years to complete.

However, because the pagoda had a brick exterior and earthen core, it gradually collapsed after more than 50 years. During the Chang'an period of Tang (701-704 AD), it was rebuilt into a seven-story square multi-story hollow brick pagoda using blue bricks.

During the Dali period of Tang (778-779 AD), it was rebuilt into a ten-story pagoda. Later, after war damage, seven stories remained.

During the Changxing period of Five Dynasties (930-933 AD), the 2nd year of Chenghua, Ming (1466), and the 32nd year of Wanli, Ming (1604 AD), maintenance was carried out based on the style rebuilt during Wu Zetian's time.

Especially during the Wanli period maintenance, face bricks were added to the damaged pagoda body, wrapping the entire Tang pagoda, and the pagoda ladder was rebuilt inside, forming the basic form of today's Giant Wild Goose Pagoda.

The existing Giant Wild Goose Pagoda is a square multi-story pagoda of brick-wood structure, composed of three parts: pagoda foundation, pagoda body, and pagoda finial.

The total height of the pagoda is 64.5 m; the pagoda foundation is 4.2 m high, about 48.7 m long north-south and about 45.7 m long east-west; the bottom side of the pagoda body is 25.5 m,呈 square cone shape; the pagoda finial is 4.87 m high.

The interior plane of the pagoda is also square, with wooden floor slabs on each story, wooden ladders set up, allowing spiral climbing to the top.

The thickness of each floor is: first story 10.36 m, second 7.37 m, third 7.15 m, fourth 6.65 m, fifth 6.7 m, sixth 6.4 m, seventh 5.2 m (pagoda top thickness not counted). The Giant Wild Goose Pagoda body imitates wooden structure.

After the Ming dynasty brick wrapping, the facade is divided into imitated wooden multi-story style using pilasters; the first and second stories have nine bays on each side, the third and fourth stories have seven bays, and the fifth, sixth, and seventh stories have five bays.

The central bay of the first story's front face has an arched door, and the central bay of each face on each subsequent story has an arched window.

The wall surfaces of each story of the pagoda body use brick flat columns and architraves, with large capitals on top of the columns, and brick-hidden architraves and column-head capitals between columns.

Each story is divided by corbelled eaves, with diamond-shaped tooth bricks laid under the eaves. The pagoda top stacks treasure vases and gourds as the conclusion of the entire pagoda.

On both sides inside the south arched door of the pagoda body, two stone steles are embedded in brick niches: the 'Preface to the Sacred Teachings of the Great Tang Tripitaka' and the 'Record of the Sacred Teachings of the Great Tang Tripitaka', with the west stone door lintel inscribed by the famous Tang calligrapher Chu Suiliang.

The stone carvings on both arched door lintels include Tang-carved architectural patterns and Buddha statues and other line carvings, with rigorous composition, vigorous and smooth lines, and meticulous content, legendarily出自 the hands of famous Tang painters Yan Liben and Yuchi Yiseng, making them important materials for studying Tang dynasty Chinese architectural forms.

The Small Wild Goose Pagoda, also called the Jianfu Temple Pagoda, is located in the Jianfu Temple south of present-day Xi'an.

The pagoda has a slender and soft form, with a simple and unadorned style, making it a representative work of Tang dynasty multi-eave pagodas. It was designated as one of the first batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 1961.

Jianfu Temple was originally called Xianfu Temple, first built in the 1st year of Wenming, Emperor Ruizong of Tang (684 AD), and renamed Jianfu Temple in the 1st year of Tianshou (690 AD).

During the Jinglong period of Emperor Zhongzong of Tang (707-709 AD), a separate pagoda courtyard was established in Anrenfang south of Jianfu Temple, and a brick pagoda was built as the Jianfu Temple Pagoda.

This pagoda was called the Wild Goose Pagoda in Tang times, also called Jianfu Pagoda in Ming times, and the name 'Small Wild Goose Pagoda' only appeared in Qing times.

During the late Tang wars, Jianfu Temple was repeatedly damaged, with the temple destroyed and only the Small Wild Goose Pagoda preserved.

According to文献 records from the Yuanyou period of Song (1086-1094 AD), by that time Jianfu Temple had moved from the original Kaihuafang into the pagoda courtyard in Anrenfang, becoming integrated with the Small Wild Goose Pagoda.

In the 6th year of Zhenghe, Emperor Huizong of Song (1116), the severely weathered pagoda eaves and corners were repaired and whitewashed, with traces of whitewashing still visible on the pagoda body today.

The Ming and Qing dynasties carried out multiple repairs to Jianfu Temple and the Small Wild Goose Pagoda; there were five large-scale renovations in the Ming dynasty, basically preserving the original layout.

The Small Wild Goose Pagoda was originally a square fifteen-story multi-eave multi-story hollow blue brick pagoda.

In the 23rd year of Chenghua, Emperor Xianzong of Ming (1487), an earthquake caused a vertical crack from the top to the bottom; in the 34th year of Jiajing, Emperor Shizong of Ming (1556), an earthquake caused the top two stories to collapse, leaving 13 stories existing today.

Measured in 1989, the total pagoda height is 43.395 m, with a bottom side length of 11.38 m, and the ratio of height to bottom side is about 100:26. The Small Wild Goose Pagoda consists of two parts: the base and the pagoda body.

The existing base is a brick square platform, with a vertical-shaft underground palace under the base. The base is a brick square platform, with an arched door on each of the north and south sides, bluestone door lintels, and bluestone steps under the arched doors.

The stone door frames are covered with exquisite Tang line carvings, especially the celestial being offering images on the door lintels, with very high artistic value.

The brick gate tower closely attached to the pagoda body outside the north arched door of the bottom story was added in the Qing dynasty.

On the south side of the pagoda base, there is a Qing dynasty stone gate archway, with the south额 inscribed 'Wanhui Zhan'en' and the north额 inscribed 'Bu'er Famen'.

Above the base is the pagoda body; the first story of the pagoda body is relatively tall, and the heights above the second story gradually decrease, with the pagoda body slightly curved from bottom to top, both solemn and graceful.

The first story of the Small Wild Goose Pagoda body has door openings on the north and south for access; both north and south door frames are made of bluestone, with the stone door lintels line-carved with offering celestial beings and decorative patterns with typical Buddhist meanings such as vines, auspicious clouds, and kalaviṅka birds, reflecting the artistic style of the early Tang period.

The pagoda body structure is single-wall hollow, with wooden-structured floors inside and wooden ladders spiraling up to the top.

The pagoda walls have no columns or architraves; each story has brick eaves, with corbelled bricks in the eave part, interspersed with two layers of diamond-shaped tooth bricks to strengthen the eave line.

The outer wall of the first story of the pagoda was wrapped with bricks in the Ming dynasty. On the left and right of the pagoda windows, low flat seats are built with corbelled bricks, different from the form of general multi-eave pagodas.

The width of the pagoda body gradually decreases from bottom to top, becoming more收分 upward, forming a natural entasis and presenting a graceful and smooth curve outline. Each story of the pagoda has semicircular arched doors on the north and south.

The Xingjiao Temple Xuanzang Pagoda is located in the West Ci'en Pagoda Courtyard of Xingjiao Temple, Xiwei Village, Duqu Town, Chang'an District, Xi'an City.

It is the earliest existing multi-story square brick pagoda in China, and also the earliest existing Tang dynasty imitated-wooden-structure multi-story brick pagoda.

It was designated as one of the first batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 1961. After Master Xuanzang passed away in the 1st year of Linde, Emperor Gaozong of Tang (664 AD), he was initially buried at Bailuyuan.

In the 2nd year of Zongzhang (669 AD), he was reburied here, and a temple was built and a pagoda constructed in his memory. After the temple was completed, it suffered repeated wars and was repaired in various dynasties.

In the late 19th century, during the Tongzhi period of Qing, all temple halls were burned by war fires, with only three spirit pagodas surviving.

The pagoda body is a square five-story multi-story solid brick pagoda, with a total height of about 21 m and a bottom side length of 5.2 m. The bottom story has a niche room on the south side, enshrining a clay statue of Master Xuanzang.

The north wall embeds a stone stele inscription of the 'Great Tang Tripitaka Great Universal Awakening Master Pagoda Inscription' from the 4th year of Kaicheng, Tang (839 AD), hence also called the Great Universal Awakening Pagoda.

Because it is a tomb pagoda, except for the bottom story, the upper four stories cannot be climbed. The first story of the pagoda body, after later repairs, is plain brick wall with no imitated wooden components remaining.

Above the second story, the pagoda walls are made in imitated wooden structure, with three bays on each side, using brick-hidden pilasters, architraves, and pubanfang (flat beam), with bracket sets of 'one dou three sheng' projecting heads, no inter-column bracket sets.

The pagoda top is built with flat bricks converging to a point, with a treasure-vase-style pagoda finial (added during repairs in the 1920s and 1930s).

Between stories, the pagoda body uses eleven layers of flat bricks for corbelled eaves, with the first and third layers being diamond-shaped tooth bricks; the projecting eave bricks gradually increase, making the corbelling present an inward-curving arc, which is the artistic style of Tang dynasty corbelled pagoda eaves, with wind chimes hanging at the eave corners.

In addition to the imitated wooden structure style, this pagoda also uses half bracket sets (door arches) on the column heads at the inner corners of the pagoda; among works of the same period, this practice is only found in the west corridor bracket sets of Horyu-ji Temple in Japan's Nara period.

After the Tang dynasty, most pagoda construction used Song and Liao brick-built tomb chambers, so this door arch system is rarely used on pagodas, making it the earliest existing example of this practice in Chinese architectural history.

The Xuanzang Pagoda stands in the center of the Xingjiao Temple pagoda courtyard, facing south with its back to north.

On its left and right sides are two three-story pagodas, which are the relic pagodas of his disciples Kuiji (Great Master Kuiji) and Yuance (Great Master Yuance of Ximing Temple).

The Kuiji Pagoda was first built in the 1st year of Yongchun (682 AD) and rebuilt in the 3rd year of Dahe (829 AD). This pagoda is a three-level square multi-story brick pagoda, about 7 m high with a bottom side length of 2 m.

The Yuance Pagoda was built when the relics were moved from Fengde Temple on Zhongnan Mountain in the 5th year of Zhenghe, Northern Song (1115); this pagoda's form is basically the same as the Kuiji Pagoda.

Both pagodas were designated as one of the first batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 1961. The Xiangji Temple Pagoda is located in Xiangji Temple on Shenheyuan at the foot of Zhongnan Mountain, Chang'an District, Xi'an City.

It was built by Pure Land sect disciples to commemorate Master Shandao, hence also called the Shandao Pagoda. It was designated as the fifth batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 2001. Xiangji Temple and its pagoda were built in the 2nd year of Shenlong, Tang (706 AD).

In the 3rd year of Taiping Xingguo, Song (978 AD), it was renamed Kaihe Temple, but soon restored its original name. During the Qianlong period of Qing, partial repairs were carried out on the temple and pagoda.

The Shandao Pagoda is located at the northwest corner inside Xiangji Temple, and is the oldest existing pagoda in the temple.

The Buddhist pagoda was originally a thirteen-level multi-eave imitated-wooden-structure hollow brick pagoda; due to a late Tang earthquake, the pagoda top fell, leaving only 11 stories existing today.

This pagoda has a square plane, brick single-wall structure, with a wooden ladder inside leading to the top, and the pagoda finial is a treasure-vase gourd top.

The bottom story is relatively tall, the second story suddenly becomes low and short, and the height and width gradually decrease story by story, with the pagoda walls slightly收分, reaching a side length of 5.1 m at the tenth story.

Measured, the residual height from the pagoda body to the pagoda top is 28.95 m, with a total height including the base of 31.00 m. The bottom story has doors on all four sides; on the south door lintel, there is an inscription of 'Nirvana Shengshi' carved during the Qianlong period.

Inside the pagoda body, the Diamond Sutra is engraved in regular script on all four sides, with elegant and graceful characters and vigorous strokes.

The basic form of the Xiangji Temple Pagoda is a multi-eave pagoda, but it also has multi-story characteristics; each story interface of the pagoda body has corbelled brick eaves, densely distributed along the height, showing the characteristics of multi-eave brick pagodas.

The bottom story of the pagoda body is plain and unadorned, and the wall surfaces above each story have protruding imitated wooden structures; each face has 4 square pilasters, dividing the wall surface into three bays, with one architrave on top of the columns, capitals on the column heads, and corbelled eaves with two layers of diamond-shaped tooth bricks between the capitals; each story's central bay has an arched niche, and the side bays have brick columns with red-painted lattice windows in the middle.

These decorative treatments also show certain characteristics of simulating wooden multi-story buildings.

关键特征 / Key Features

  • 陕西是全国保留古塔较多的省份之一
  • 截至2006年年底
  • 陕西境内现存不同时期的古塔共287座
  • 从时间与空间分布来看
  • 陕西唐代古塔主要集中在京城长安附近
  • 宋塔则主要集中在通往北部、西北部的军事要道上
  • 明代佛塔主要集中于陕西北部
  • 清代分布地区稍显平均
  • 从建筑材料来看
  • 砖塔最多
  • 为164座
  • 占全部古塔的57.1%
  • 其余类型分别有石塔102座、砖石塔4座、铁塔5座、土塔10座、木塔1座、琉璃塔1座
  • 陕西古塔种类齐全
  • 造型多样

91. 宋明佛塔（柏山寺塔、崇文宝塔、法门寺塔、中王堡木塔、千佛铁塔、千佛琉璃塔、鸠摩罗什舍利塔）

Song-Ming Pagodas (Baishan Temple Pagoda, Chongwen Pagoda, Famen Temple Pagoda, Zhongwangbao Wooden Pagoda, Thousand-Buddha Iron Pagoda, Thousand-Buddha Glazed Pagoda, Kumarajiva Relic Pagoda)
宋、明 佛塔建筑 全国重点文物保护单位 延安市富县、咸阳市泾阳县、宝鸡市扶风县、咸阳市三原县、咸阳市、延安市、西安市户县

富县柏山寺塔位于延安市南部富县直罗镇柏山半山之际，虽然从史料未能清晰知道该塔始建年代为唐代还是宋代，但是从塔的造型及塔上造像艺术风格看，该塔应为宋代建筑。

1992年公布为陕西省重点文物保护单位。明弘治本《延安府志》卷五载：柏山禅寺，在（麟州）城西一百二十里。柏山寺塔即为该寺的标志塔，现寺院已无存。

柏山寺塔也名直罗塔，为八边十一层密檐楼阁式砖塔，通高43.3米。塔正南辟有拱形塔门，内有塔室，为四方形，边长2.3米，至第二层底部以砖叠涩收分。塔身二层以上每层辟四券门、四方龛，上下层门、龛不相错。

第二层至券门两侧有直条棂格窗，有的塔门又作小拱形龛，内置佛教造像，如天王、罗汉等。富县文管办已从塔身取下9尊造像收藏，现塔院内仍存少数造像。从造像风格看，为宋代艺术的珍品，弥足珍贵。柏山寺塔为砖木结构的典型建筑。

塔身各层均以砖叠涩出檐，有檐椽、飞子，檐下饰砖雕斗栱，斗栱为宋式五铺作制式，柱头铺作为五铺作出双抄，补间铺作由于攒档较为密集，因此采用五铺作偷心造的做法。

整体感觉比例、尺度十分和谐。二层以上每层皆有平座与栏杆，单边三开间，方形壁柱、额枋雕刻清晰。泾阳崇文宝塔位于咸阳市泾阳县崇文乡东太平村，是明代砖塔建筑中现存规模最大，保存最完整的仿木楼阁式砖塔，为明塔的典型代表，同时是全国现存最高的砖塔。

2001年公布为第五批全国重点文物保护单位。崇文宝塔，世称泾阳塔，始建于唐，1556年毁于地震。

明万历十九年（1591年）重建，至万历三十六年（1608年）竣工，由明刑部尚书、本县人李世达主持，南京镇江工匠施工所建。

塔前尚存明万历甲寅年（1614年）泾阳县壬子科崇文宝塔题名记、万历丙午季春泾阳县崇文宝塔题名记、泾阳县崇文宝塔乙酉科题名记、清顺治十六年（1659年）铁佛崇文宝塔寺常住田供众记等明清石碑四通。

据铁佛崇文宝塔寺常住田供众记记载，该塔为倡导泾阳、三原、高陵三县学童努力向学而建。清同治元年（1862年）崇文寺院被焚，仅存塔与五间山门。

泾阳崇文宝塔是八边十三层楼阁式空心砖塔，通高87.218米。塔基为须弥座，底层每边长9米。

塔身一层作重檐，南向辟拱券门，额题崇文宝塔四字，左右两侧有竖书，塔身其余各面设佛龛。二层以上每层辟四券门、四佛龛，上下层门、龛逐层依次相错，龛内均置明代石雕佛像一尊，或立或坐。

塔身层间叠涩出檐，施仿木形式椽头、斗栱、额枋、菱角牙子与平座。额枋上饰砖雕荷花、瑞兽、飞天、寿星等图案。塔壁角倚圆柱，檐角缀风铃。二、四、六层增设垂莲柱，将每面分为三间。

塔顶安置4.19米高铜质宝瓶式塔刹。塔内为穿心式结构，塔壁内为券顶式环廊，形成类似于现代高层建筑筒中筒的建筑结构形式。砖阶梯在中心柱中盘旋而上，进入上一层环形拱廊，再由塔心柱中穿插而上。砖石发券的拱廊做法十分考究，反映了当时高超的施工技术。这种层与层间，外壁与中心柱结为一体的结构构造方法，增强了整体的抗裂性能。

塔心柱每面除楼梯口外，均辟券龛，周围圈以城垛式护墙。1979年在塔顶部发现8尊鎏金铜佛像、菩萨像和天王像等，造型逼真生动，为明代珍品。

扶风法门寺塔位于陕西省宝鸡市扶风县城以北10公里处的法门镇上，是我国最著名的佛教古刹真身宝塔之一。法门寺因舍利而置塔，因塔而建寺，原名阿育王寺。释迦牟尼佛灭度后，遗体火化结成舍利。

公元前3世纪，阿育王统一印度后，为弘扬佛法，将佛的舍利分成84000份，使诸鬼神于南阎浮提，分送世界各国建塔供奉。中国有19处，法门寺为第五处。法门寺塔，阁创始于东汉桓、灵年间，原为四层木结构塔，高约27米。

1569年关中大地震，法门寺塔被震塌。

明万历七年（1579年）神宗赐银数万两建塔，历时30年在明万历三十七年（1609年）建成，仍仿木塔形式重修为60余米高的八棱十三级砖塔，雕檐金顶，做工细致。

该塔在1976年8月16日的四川松潘、平武地震后，在塔底的西南一侧出现了自下而上的，长约8米的裂缝，缝最宽处约2厘米。

由于没有及时采取措施，进行维修和抗震加固处理，致使该塔在1981年8月的一场大雨中溜倒了一半，造成了不可弥补的损失。仅剩下的一半，突兀孤立，岌岌可危，当地有关部门于1985年8月将残塔拆除。

现在的新塔仍然是按明塔进行原样重建，2014年建成。新建的塔为砖砌楼阁式，平面八角十三层，高60余米。第一层塔身八面，南面塔门，上有真身宝塔四字的石匾。其余四面为八卦：乾、艮、巽、坤等字样，以记方位。塔身的第一层檐下，用砖刻制出垂爪柱、帐幔和斗栱、椽子等构件。

从第八到第八层，檐下均刻出额枋、斗栱，以叠涩出檐。八层以上各层仅作叠涩出檐，而无斗栱和其他构件。第十三层做成了八角形圆盖。塔刹为铜覆体、宝珠。

塔的第二层至第十二层共有佛像龛88个，每龛置铜佛或菩萨造像1至3尊，共计104尊，大者形同真人，小者只有0.2米左右。塔上的造像庄重肃穆，铸造技术精湛。

在20世纪中国现代考古记载中，法门寺地宫是最重大的发现之一。

1987年4月，2000多件大唐皇室重宝簇拥着举世无双的佛指舍利横空出世，法门寺在一夜之间成为世界关注的焦点。

而从建筑学的角度来看，法门寺塔的地宫形制有着突出的特点。法门寺塔地宫的形制与迄今所知中外各国的佛塔地宫有很大的差异。

地宫前、中、后三室不是同时构筑的，唐高宗时期重建的法门寺塔地宫只有位于塔基中心方座正中的后室一室，中室和前室分别是在唐中宗和唐懿宗时期改造地宫甬道和隧道形成的，因此地宫既不是事先按照皇帝陵墓的规制设计和建造的，也不是咸通十五年封闭时按照密教仪轨统一布置的唐密曼荼罗。

中王堡木塔原名文峰木塔，因位于咸阳市三原县安乐镇中王堡村，也称作中王堡木塔。该塔结构简明，造型秀丽，为陕西仅存的一座木塔。1957年公布为陕西省第二批重点文物保护单位。

该塔始建于明万历二十二年（1594年），清康熙十九年（1680年）曾进行过大规模整修，民国二十九年（1940年）再次修缮。

中王堡木塔为六边三层四檐楼阁式木塔，通高约20米。塔基为砖石砌六边形，底边长13米，高4米。木塔塔身木质，自地至顶用6根通柱，底层柱包砌在墙内，施周回廊构成下檐。

二、三层出檐由平座永定柱并挑枋吊柱支承挑檐，不施斗栱，挑檐逐层收进，上层柱脚落在下层柱脚抱头梁上。塔身每面辟格子窗，塔顶构架由额枋及数层抹角梁叠架而成，雷公柱落在太平梁上，如亭阁式做法。

六角攒尖顶，置宝珠式塔刹，每层每面檐角翘起，有正脊和戗脊、脊兽，檐角下有套兽，兽头下缀风铃。塔台与前大殿之间以拱桥相连，为登塔通道，底层西北方向有门，塔内设扶梯，可拾级而上到二、三层。因应木质结构特点，木塔腰身显得有些粗壮，不似砖塔比例修长。

千佛铁塔，本名千佛塔，因位于离咸阳市区约10公里处的北杜镇福昌寺内，又名北杜铁塔、福昌寺塔。它是我国古建遗存中保护完整且最高的一座铁塔，因其塔身遍布有数以千计的大小佛像，故称千佛铁塔。

2013年公布为全国第七批重点文物保护单位。

塔始建于明万历十八年（1590年），塔为八边九层密檐楼阁式铁塔，通高约21米，底边长2.42米。

塔基为砖砌八边形，高3.08米，檐下饰砖雕额枋、角饰圆形倚柱，上有转角垂莲柱。塔基南北均辟券门，内设砖阶梯，两门均可到达铁塔一层之外。塔基南券门上方嵌楷书千佛塔三字铁匾，长1.02米，宽0.51米。

匾额上方横书延安府清涧县训导聘室人王氏杨氏，落款大明万历三十八年岁次戌吉日立，铸塔人为南书房行走太监杜茂。塔身材料为外铁内砖，底层南、东、西三面辟圆拱门，有门框、楣、槛（原装铁门已毁），塔身一层东南、东北、西北、西南各面铸四大天王像，神态各异，栩栩如生。塔身二层多处铸有铭文。层间做六铺作双下昂斗栱、并出斜栱以及仿砖木出檐。

一层至九层外部全部铁铸，塔内砖砌壁内折上楼梯，可至塔顶。各层外壁铸铁佛多尊及荷花、芭蕉等花草和凤凰、仙鹤、麒麟、奔马、老虎、犀牛望月、猴子摘桃等动物图案。各层依据需要和层高等实际情况，辟不同数量的券门或望孔，故当地有民谚：铁塔寺，九层子，八棱子，二十四个窗门子。华盖式八角攒尖顶，宝瓶式塔刹。

千佛琉璃塔为陕西省仅见的一座琉璃塔。该塔原位于延安市宝塔区甘谷驿镇唐家坪村南山坡，1985年迁至清凉山仙石洞西侧。

2013年公布为全国第七批重点文物保护单位。

千佛琉璃塔建于明崇祯二年（1629年），塔为八角形，七层仿木楼阁式实心琉璃塔，残高6.25米，底层直径1.15米。

塔刹原为铜质圆形，后被盗。塔通体以孔雀蓝琉璃为主，间以黑、黄、褐、赭、绿等色琉璃砖砌筑。内部以土坯填实，以柏木桩固定。塔身外部每层隐出倚柱、额枋、屋檐、斗栱及浮雕各式造像。塔身底层每面饰一佛二天王，间以小佛像41个；

二层每面饰一佛二菩萨、二飞天，间以小佛像56个；三层饰形态各异的飞龙，其中一面为二龙盘旋，间以小佛像32个；四层饰凤凰、朱雀、麒麟、鹿、马等瑞兽灵物，四周间以小佛像11或者15个，下为海水；

五层以上每面均饰一座佛，佛的头部和两侧有五排46个小佛像；第六层每面上部中间有黄琉璃坐佛，下部和左右两侧有五排39个小佛像；第七层每面的中间上部有一黄琉璃坐佛，下部和左右两侧有五排29个小佛像。

千佛琉璃塔每层均装有出檐，檐下有额枋、柱头、斗栱，檐脊上饰有牡丹、忍冬、如意云纹等。檐基两端饰有吻与蛟龙。塔身第五层有铭文崇祯二年八月十一日造成，山西汾州府匠人侯大阳21字。塔身七层正中保存有长33厘米、宽13厘米、高14厘米的柏木舍利子函盒。

盒内有红绸小袋，内装5小卷经卷，以及用珍珠、玛瑙制成的12粒舍利子。盒内还有天启通宝铜钱一枚，重圈铭文铜镜一块。盒底部压有线装太上诸仙经典训诫一册。

草堂寺鸠摩罗什舍利塔位于西安市户县草堂镇草堂营村草堂寺内，是后秦高僧鸠摩罗什的舍利塔。

2001年公布为第五批全国重点文物保护单位。

鸠摩罗什为天竺僧人，后秦弘始三年（公元401年）入住草堂寺，主持译经工作，并与真谛、玄奘、不空并称中国佛教四大译家。

公元413年，鸠摩罗什于草堂寺涅槃火化，舍利保存在该寺。

鸠摩罗什舍利塔建于唐代，为单层亭阁式仿木八角舍利石塔，塔高2.44米，底座呈方形，边长1.69米。

因全塔用玉白、砖青、墨黑、乳黄、淡红、浅蓝、赭紫、深灰等八种颜色的玉石雕刻镶嵌而成，所以俗称八宝玉石塔。舍利塔由塔座、塔身、塔顶、塔刹四部分组成，上下共十二层次，也有简单地称其为八面十二层的。《中国文物图集·陕西分册》记载：一层圆盘状，沿盘面浮雕须弥山及佛、兽等；

以上几个层次均呈圆形，依次雕水波、二重流云、水波、流云，上托八角塔身。塔座是由山、海浪以及海浪升腾出的祥云组成的须弥座；塔身是仿木建筑的八面形塔体，以浮雕的手法，雕出倚柱、板门、直棂窗及阑额等；塔顶为四角攒尖式，出椽头、屋脊和瓦垄；

塔刹由刹座、仰覆莲及扁圆宝珠构成，形制也具独特风格，在简洁的刹座上，刻出巨大的似莲瓣形花叶，承托巨大的扁圆宝珠。

— ENGLISH —

The Baishan Temple Pagoda in Fuxian County is located on the mountainside of Baishan Mountain, Zhiluo Town, Fuxian County, southern Yan'an City.

Although historical records do not clearly indicate whether the pagoda was built in the Tang or Song dynasty, judging from the pagoda's form and the artistic style of the statues on the pagoda, it should be a Song dynasty building.

It was designated as a Shaanxi Provincial Key Cultural Heritage Site in 1992.

The Hongzhi edition of the 'Yan'an Prefecture Gazetteer' (Ming dynasty) volume 5 records: 'Baishan Chan Temple is 120 li west of (Linzhou) city.' The Baishan Temple Pagoda was the landmark pagoda of this temple; the temple no longer exists today.

The Baishan Temple Pagoda, also called the Zhiluo Pagoda, is an eight-sided eleven-story multi-eave multi-story brick pagoda, with a total height of 43.3 m.

An arched pagoda door is opened on the due south side, with a pagoda chamber inside, square-shaped, with a side length of 2.3 m; at the bottom of the second story, it is收分 with corbelled bricks.

Above the second story, each story of the pagoda body has four arched doors and four square niches, with the doors and niches of upper and lower stories not staggered.

On both sides of the arched doors from the second story, there are straight lattice windows; some pagoda doors are made into small arched niches, with Buddhist statues inside, such as heavenly kings and arhats.

The Fuxian County Cultural relics Management Office has removed 9 statues from the pagoda body for collection, and a few statues still remain in the pagoda courtyard. Judging from the statue style, they are precious Song dynasty art treasures, extremely valuable.

The Baishan Temple Pagoda is a typical building of brick-wood structure.

Each story of the pagoda body has corbelled eaves with bricks, with eave rafters and flying rafters; under the eaves, brick-carved bracket sets are decorated, with the bracket sets in Song-style five-puzuo format, the column-head bracket sets as five-puzuo with double double-tiao, and the inter-column bracket sets using five-puzuo touxin-zao (hidden-heart construction) due to the relatively dense spacing.

The overall proportion and scale are very harmonious. Above the second story, each story has flat seats and railings, with three bays on each side, and square wall columns and architraves clearly carved.

The Chongwen Pagoda in Jingyang is located in Dongtaiping Village, Chongwen Township, Jingyang County, Xianyang City.

It is the largest-scale and best-preserved existing imitated-wooden multi-story brick pagoda among Ming dynasty brick pagoda buildings, a typical representative of Ming pagodas, and also the tallest existing brick pagoda in China.

It was designated as the fifth batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 2001. The Chongwen Pagoda, commonly called the Jingyang Pagoda, was first built in the Tang dynasty and destroyed in the 1556 earthquake.

It was rebuilt in the 19th year of Wanli, Ming (1591) and completed in the 36th year of Wanli (1608), presided over by Li Shida, Minister of Justice of the Ming dynasty and a local of Jingyang, and constructed by craftsmen from Zhenjiang, Nanjing.

In front of the pagoda, four Ming-Qing stone steles still remain: the 'Inscription of the Renzi Section Chongwen Pagoda in Jingyang County' from the Jiayin year of Wanli, Ming (1614), the 'Inscription of the Chongwen Pagoda in Jingyang County' from the Bingwu year of Wanli in early spring, the 'Inscription of the Yiyou Section Chongwen Pagoda in Jingyang County', and the 'Record of the Permanent Farmland and Public Offerings of the Iron Buddha Chongwen Pagoda Temple' from the 16th year of Shunzhi, Qing (1659).

According to the 'Record of the Permanent Farmland and Public Offerings of the Iron Buddha Chongwen Pagoda Temple', the pagoda was built to encourage schoolchildren in Jingyang, Sanyuan, and Gaoling counties to study diligently.

In the 1st year of Tongzhi, Qing (1862), the Chongwen Temple was burned, leaving only the pagoda and five bays of the mountain gate. The Jingyang Chongwen Pagoda is an eight-sided thirteen-story multi-story hollow brick pagoda, with a total height of 87.218 m.

The pagoda base is a xumizuo (Sumeru pedestal), with each side of the bottom story 9 m long.

The first story of the pagoda body has double eaves, with an arched door on the south side, the额 inscribed with the four characters 'Chongwen Pagoda', with vertical inscriptions on the left and right sides, and Buddhist niches on the other faces of the pagoda body.

Above the second story, each story has four arched doors and four Buddhist niches, with the doors and niches staggered sequentially from upper to lower stories; each niche contains a Ming dynasty stone-carved Buddha statue, either standing or sitting.

Between stories, the pagoda body has corbelled eaves, with imitated wooden-style rafter heads, bracket sets, architraves, diamond-shaped tooth bricks, and flat seats.

The architraves are decorated with brick-carved patterns such as lotus flowers, auspicious beasts, flying apsaras, and the god of longevity. The corners of the pagoda walls have cylindrical pilasters, with wind chimes hanging at the eave corners.

On the second, fourth, and sixth stories, additional hanging lotus columns are added, dividing each face into three bays. At the top of the pagoda, a 4.19-m-high copper treasure-vase-style pagoda finial is installed.

The interior of the pagoda is a through-core structure, with vaulted ring corridors inside the pagoda walls, forming an architectural structure form similar to the 'tube-in-tube' of modern high-rise buildings.

Brick stairs spiral upward in the central column, entering the upper-story ring corridor, then穿插 upward through the central column. The arched corridor construction with brick-stone vaulting is very exquisite, reflecting the superb construction technology of the time.

This structural construction method, where the outer wall and central column are integrated between stories, enhances the overall crack resistance. Except for the stair openings, each face of the central column has arched niches, surrounded by crenellated protective walls.

In 1979, 8 gilded copper Buddha statues, bodhisattva statues, and heavenly king statues were found at the top of the pagoda, with vivid and lifelike forms, making them Ming dynasty treasures.

The Famen Temple Pagoda in Fufeng is located in Famen Town, 10 km north of Fufeng County town, Baoji City, Shaanxi Province. It is one of the most famous Buddhist ancient temple true-body pagodas in China.

Famen Temple established a pagoda because of the relics and built a temple because of the pagoda, originally named Ashoka Temple. After Sakyamuni Buddha's parinirvana, his body was cremated and formed relics.

In the 3rd century BC, after Ashoka unified India, to promote Buddhism, he divided the Buddha's relics into 84,000 portions, sending ghosts and gods to Jambudvipa to distribute them to various countries around the world for pagoda enshrinement.

There are 19 sites in China, with Famen Temple being the fifth. The Famen Temple Pagoda was first founded during the Huan and Ling reigns of the Eastern Han dynasty, originally a four-story wooden structure pagoda, about 27 m high.

In the 1569 Guanzhong earthquake, the Famen Temple Pagoda collapsed.

In the 7th year of Wanli, Ming (1579), Emperor Shenzong granted tens of thousands of taels of silver to build the pagoda; after 30 years, it was completed in the 37th year of Wanli (1609), still rebuilt in the imitation wooden pagoda form as an eight-sided thirteen-level brick pagoda over 60 m high, with carved eaves and a golden top, with meticulous workmanship.

After the Songpan-Pingwu earthquake in Sichuan on August 16, 1976, a crack appeared from bottom to top on the southwest side of the pagoda base, about 8 m long, with the widest part about 2 cm.

Because timely measures were not taken for maintenance and seismic reinforcement, the pagoda collapsed by half in a heavy rain in August 1981, causing irreparable losses. The remaining half stood isolated and precarious; the local relevant department demolished the residual pagoda in August 1985.

The current new pagoda was still rebuilt in the original Ming pagoda style, completed in 2014. The newly built pagoda is a brick multi-story structure, with an octagonal plane and thirteen stories, over 60 m high.

The first story of the pagoda body has eight faces, with a pagoda door on the south side, above which is a stone plaque with the four characters 'True Body Pagoda'. The other four faces have the Eight Trigrams: Qian, Gen, Xun, Kun, etc., to record directions.

Under the eaves of the first story of the pagoda body, components such as hanging claw columns, curtains, bracket sets, and rafters are carved with bricks. From the eighth to the eighth story, architraves and bracket sets are carved under the eaves, with corbelled eaves.

Above the eighth story, each story only has corbelled eaves without bracket sets or other components. The thirteenth story is made into an octagonal round cover. The pagoda finial is a copper覆体 and precious pearl.

From the second to the twelfth story of the pagoda, there are a total of 88 Buddha niches, each containing 1 to 3 copper Buddha or bodhisattva statues, totaling 104 statues; the large ones are life-sized, and the small ones are only about 0.2 m.

The statues on the pagoda are solemn and serene, with exquisite casting technology. In the records of modern Chinese archaeology in the 20th century, the Famen Temple underground palace is one of the most significant discoveries.

In April 1987, more than 2,000 Tang royal treasures surrounded the unparalleled Buddha finger relic, which emerged spectacularly, making Famen Temple the focus of world attention overnight.

From an architectural perspective, the underground palace form of the Famen Temple Pagoda has outstanding characteristics.

The form of the Famen Temple Pagoda underground palace is very different from the underground palaces of Buddhist pagodas in China and abroad known to date.

The front, middle, and rear chambers of the underground palace were not constructed simultaneously; the Famen Temple Pagoda underground palace rebuilt during the Gaozong period of Tang only had the rear chamber located in the center of the square base at the center of the pagoda foundation, while the middle and front chambers were formed by改造 the underground palace corridors and tunnels during the Zhongzong and Yizong periods of Tang respectively.

Therefore, the underground palace was neither designed and built in advance according to the regulations of imperial Mausoleums, nor was it a Tang Tantric mandala uniformly arranged according to Tantric rituals when sealed in the 15th year of Xiantong.

The Zhongwangbao Wooden Pagoda, originally named Wenfeng Wooden Pagoda, is also called the Zhongwangbao Wooden Pagoda because it is located in Zhongwangbao Village, Anle Town, Sanyuan County, Xianyang City.

This pagoda has a simple structure and graceful form, making it the only existing wooden pagoda in Shaanxi. It was designated as the second batch of Shaanxi Provincial Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 1957.

This pagoda was first built in the 22nd year of Wanli, Ming (1594), underwent large-scale renovation in the 19th year of Kangxi, Qing (1680), and was repaired again in the 29th year of the Republic (1940).

The Zhongwangbao Wooden Pagoda is a hexagonal three-story four-eave multi-story wooden pagoda, with a total height of about 20 m. The pagoda base is a brick-stone hexagon, with a bottom side length of 13 m and height of 4 m.

The pagoda body is wooden, using 6 through columns from ground to top; the bottom-story columns are enclosed in walls, with surrounding corridors forming the lower eaves.

The eaves of the second and third stories are supported by flat-seat yongding columns and挑枋 hanging columns, without bracket sets; the projecting eaves gradually收进 story by story, with the upper-story column feet resting on the baotou beams of the lower-story column feet.

Each face of the pagoda body has lattice windows; the pagoda top framework is formed by stacking architraves and several layers of mitered beams, with the leigong column resting on the taiping beam, like a pavilion-style construction.

It has a hexagonal pyramidal top with a treasure-vase-style pagoda finial; each story's eave corners turn upward on each face, with main ridges and diagonal ridges, ridge beasts, and套 beasts under the eave corners, with wind chimes hanging under the beast heads.

The pagoda platform and the front main hall are connected by an arch bridge, serving as the pagoda-climbing passage; there is a door in the northwest direction of the bottom story, with a ladder inside the pagoda allowing climbing to the second and third stories.

Due to the characteristics of the wooden structure, the waist of the wooden pagoda appears somewhat sturdy, not as slender in proportion as brick pagodas.

The Thousand-Buddha Iron Pagoda, originally named the Thousand-Buddha Pagoda, is also called the Beidu Iron Pagoda or Fuchang Temple Pagoda because it is located in Fuchang Temple, Beidu Town, about 10 km from the Xianyang urban area.

It is the tallest and best-preserved iron pagoda among existing ancient Chinese buildings; because its pagoda body is covered with thousands of large and small Buddha statues, it is called the Thousand-Buddha Iron Pagoda.

It was designated as the seventh batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 2013.

The pagoda was first built in the 18th year of Wanli, Ming (1590); it is an eight-sided nine-story multi-eave multi-story iron pagoda, with a total height of about 21 m and a bottom side length of 2.42 m.

The pagoda base is a brick octagon, 3.08 m high, with brick-carved architraves under the eaves, round pilasters at the corners, and corner hanging lotus columns on top.

Arched doors are opened on both the north and south of the pagoda base, with brick stairs inside, both doors allowing access to outside the first story of the iron pagoda.

Above the south arched door of the pagoda base, an iron plaque with the three characters 'Thousand-Buddha Pagoda' in regular script is embedded, 1.02 m long and 0.51 m wide.

Above the plaque, it is horizontally inscribed 'Instructor Wang's wife Yang of Qingjian County, Yan'an Prefecture', with the signature 'Erected on an auspicious day in the Wuxu year, the 38th year of Wanli, Ming', and the pagoda caster was Du Mao, a eunuch serving in the Southern Study.

The pagoda body material is iron outside and brick inside; the bottom story has round arched doors on the south, east, and west faces, with door frames, lintels, and thresholds (the original iron doors have been destroyed); on the southeast, northeast, northwest, and southwest faces of the first story, four heavenly king statues are cast, each with different expressions and lifelike.

Multiple inscriptions are cast on the second story of the pagoda body. Between stories, six-puzuo double-down-ang bracket sets, diagonal bracket sets, and imitation brick-wood eaves are made.

From the first to the ninth story, the exterior is entirely cast iron; inside the pagoda, brick walls have折上 stairs allowing climbing to the top.

The outer walls of each story cast multiple Buddha statues and animal patterns such as lotus, banana and other flowers and plants, and phoenixes, cranes, qilin, galloping horses, tigers, rhinos gazing at the moon, and monkeys picking peaches.

Each story opens different numbers of arched doors or viewing holes according to actual needs and story heights, hence the local folk proverb: 'Iron Pagoda Temple, nine stories, eight sides, twenty-four window doors.' It has a canopy-style octagonal pyramidal top and a treasure-vase-style pagoda finial.

The Thousand-Buddha glazed Pagoda is the only glazed pagoda seen in Shaanxi Province.

This pagoda was originally located on the southern hillside of Tangjiaping Village, Ganguyi Town, Baota District, Yan'an City, and was moved to the west side of the Xianren Stone Cave on Qingliang Mountain in 1985.

It was designated as the seventh batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 2013.

The Thousand-Buddha glazed Pagoda was built in the 2nd year of Chongzhen, Ming (1629); it is an octagonal, seven-story imitation wooden multi-story solid glazed pagoda, with a residual height of 6.25 m and a bottom diameter of 1.15 m.

The pagoda finial was originally copper and round, but was later stolen. The entire pagoda body is mainly peacock-blue glazed, interspersed with black, yellow, brown, reddish-brown, green and other colored glazed bricks.

The interior is filled with adobe and fixed with cypress wooden piles. The exterior of each story of the pagoda body has hidden pilasters, architraves, eaves, bracket sets, and relief-carved various statues.

The bottom story of the pagoda body is decorated with one Buddha and two heavenly kings on each face, interspersed with 41 small Buddha statues; the second story has one Buddha, two bodhisattvas, and two flying apsaras on each face, interspersed with 56 small Buddha statues; the third story is decorated with flying dragons of various forms, with one face showing two dragons spiraling, interspersed with 32 small Buddha statues; the fourth story is decorated with auspicious beasts and spirit creatures such as phoenixes, vermilion birds, qilin, deer, and horses, with 11 or 15 small Buddha statues interspersed around, and seawater below; above the fifth story, each face is decorated with one Buddha, with five rows of 46 small Buddha statues on the head and both sides of the Buddha; the sixth story has a yellow glazed sitting Buddha in the upper middle of each face, with five rows of 39 small Buddha statues below and on both left and right sides; the seventh story has a yellow glazed sitting Buddha in the upper middle of each face, with five rows of 29 small Buddha statues below and on both left and right sides.

Each story of the Thousand-Buddha glazed Pagoda is equipped with projecting eaves, with architraves, column heads, and bracket sets under the eaves; the eave ridges are decorated with patterns such as peonies, honeysuckle, and ruyi cloud patterns.

Both ends of the eave base are decorated with chi (mythical beasts) and flood dragons. The fifth story of the pagoda body has an inscription: 'Built on the 11th day of the 8th month of the 2nd year of Chongzhen, by craftsman Hou Dayang from Fenzhou Prefecture, Shanxi'—21 characters.

In the center of the seventh story of the pagoda body, a cypress wood relic box 33 cm long, 13 cm wide, and 14 cm high is preserved. Inside the box, there is a red silk small bag containing 5 small scrolls of scriptures and 12 relics made of pearls and agate.

Inside the box, there is also one Tianqi Tongbao copper coin and one double-circle inscribed bronze mirror. At the bottom of the box, a thread-bound volume of 'Taishang Immortals Classic Precepts' is pressed.

The Kumarajiva relic Pagoda at Caotang Temple is located in Caotang Temple, Caotangying Village, Caotang Town, Huxian County, Xi'an City. It is the relic pagoda of the Later Qin eminent monk Kumarajiva.

It was designated as the fifth batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 2001.

Kumarajiva was an Indian monk who入住 Caotang Temple in the 3rd year of Hongshi, Later Qin (401 AD), presiding over scripture translation work, and together with Paramartha, Xuanzang, and Amoghavajra, is known as one of the four great translators of Chinese Buddhism.

In 413 AD, Kumarajiva attained nirvana and was cremated at Caotang Temple, with his relics preserved in this temple.

The Kumarajiva relic Pagoda was built in the Tang dynasty; it is a single-story pavilion-style imitation wooden octagonal relic stone pagoda, 2.44 m high, with a square base 1.69 m on each side.

Because the entire pagoda is carved and inlaid with jade stones of eight colors—jade white, brick blue, ink black, cream yellow, light red, light blue, reddish-brown purple, and dark gray—it is commonly called the Eight Treasures Jade Stone Pagoda.

The relic pagoda consists of four parts: pagoda base, pagoda body, pagoda top, and pagoda finial, with a total of twelve layers up and down, also simply called the eight-sided twelve-layer.

The 'Chinese Cultural relics Atlas: Shaanxi Volume' records: 'The first layer is disc-shaped, with relief carvings of Mount Sumeru and Buddhas and beasts along the disc surface; the above several layers are all circular, sequentially carved with water waves, double-layer flowing clouds, water waves, and flowing clouds, supporting the octagonal pagoda body.' The pagoda base is a xumizuo composed of mountains, sea waves, and auspicious clouds rising from the sea waves; the pagoda body is an octagonal pagoda body imitating wooden architecture, using relief techniques to carve out pilasters, panel doors, lattice windows, and architraves; the pagoda top is a four-corner pyramidal style, with rafter heads, roof ridges, and tile rows; the pagoda finial consists of a finial base, upturned and downturned lotus, and a flat round precious pearl, with a unique style; on the simple finial base, huge lotus-petal-like flower leaves are carved, supporting the huge flat round precious pearl.

关键特征 / Key Features

  • 富县柏山寺塔位于延安市南部富县直罗镇柏山半山之际
  • 虽然从史料未能清晰知道该塔始建年代
  • 为唐代还是宋代
  • 但是从塔的造型及塔上造像艺术风格看
  • 该塔应为宋代建筑
  • 1992年公布为陕西省重点文物保护单位
  • 明弘治本延安府志卷五载
  • 柏山禅寺
  • 在麟州城西一百二十里
  • 柏山寺塔即为该寺的标志塔
  • 现寺院已无存
  • 柏山寺塔也名直罗塔
  • 为八边十一层密檐楼阁式砖塔
  • 通高43.3米
  • 塔正南辟有拱形塔门

92. 石窟（彬县大佛寺石窟、慈善寺石窟、富县石泓寺石窟、米脂万佛洞）

Grottoes (Bin County Great Buddha Temple Grottoes, Cishan Temple Grottoes, Fuxian Shihong Temple Grottoes, Mizhi Wanfo Cave)
南北朝、隋、唐、宋 石窟建筑 全国重点文物保护单位 咸阳市彬县、宝鸡市麟游县、延安市富县、榆林市米脂县

陕西是我国石窟发展比较发达的地区之一，以晚期石窟为主。目前共发现北魏至明、清时期石窟350余处，其中比较重要的石窟：北朝9处，隋、唐、五代32处，宋、金、元73处。

陕西石窟中大型窟少，小型窟多；群体窟少，单体窟多；大体量造像少，小体量造像多；北朝、隋、唐石窟少，宋代石窟较多；长期连续造窟的窟少，时代单一的窟多；

关中及陕南的窟少，陕北的窟多；题材组合复杂的少，题材简单的多。北朝石窟有宜君县的秦家河、花石崖、福地、苜蓿沟、彭村，安塞县的剑化寺、云山品寺，黄陵县的双龙香坊，甘泉县的老君寺，彬县的大佛寺等处，北魏时代占多数。

这个时期石窟的龛、窟难分，规模较小，佛龛进深很浅，面宽与高度多在1米左右。佛龛多为拱形或尖拱形，有的龛侧饰连结柱。造像主要为一佛二菩萨，或有飞天和胡服供养人。

开凿于西魏大统元年（公元535年）的宜君福地石窟，除刻有佛教造像外，还雕有道教天尊像，发愿文中的供养者称道士、道民，是迄今发现最早有纪年的佛、道合龛造像。

隋、唐石窟众多，有蓝田之佛寺重兴，铜川的金锁关，宜君的焦寨，安塞的大佛寺、黑泉驿，富县的石泓寺、川庄，甘泉的孟家河、方家河、刘老庄，吴旗的石空寺，洛川的史家河，韩城的七佛洞、开化寺、朝阳洞，佳县的玉皇寺，靖边的千佛寺等处。

宋、金、元各代石窟主要分布在陕北延安市，这与宋王朝大力倡导佛教以及宋与西夏之间的战事有着密切关系。宋太祖建隆元年（公元960年），太祖继位数月，即解除后周毁佛之令，佛寺重兴。

尤其以安塞、甘泉、子长、富县、延安等处最为密集，不仅石窟数量多，而且技法纯熟，题材也更趋世俗化，尤以子长县钟山石窟、富县阁子头石窟、黄陵县双龙石窟以及延安市万佛洞石窟最具代表性。

彬县大佛寺石窟位于彬县城西10公里处的泾河南岸的水帘洞乡大佛寺村附近的清凉山上，是陕西省境内规模最大的石窟群，也是中国现存初唐时期和盛唐时期规模最大、最为精美的石窟群之一。

1988年公布为全国第三批重点文物保护单位。

大佛寺石窟始凿于南北朝时期，大规模开凿于唐初，贞观二年（公元628年）基本建成，原名应福寺，乃唐太宗为了给对抗薛举、薛仁杲大战中阵亡将士应福而建。北宋仁宗皇帝为其养母刘太后举国庆寿时，改名庆寿寺。

人们因其佛像高大雄伟，从明景泰年间俗称大佛寺并沿用。大佛寺石窟群坐南面北，依山凿窟，雕石成像，沿东西方向排列在陡峭垂直的崖面上。

现存大小窟龛361个，其中洞窟107个，佛龛254个，有造像的洞窟共19个，造像1498躯，错落有致地分布于约400米长的立体崖面上，分为大佛窟、千佛洞、罗汉洞（佛洞）、丈八佛窟、僧房窟五部分。

石窟群以大佛洞为全寺的中心，可以分为三大部分：中部、西部、东部。中部为整个石窟群的主体部分，包括大佛洞及紧邻两侧的千佛洞和罗汉洞，共12窟，称之为中崖，即明镜台；西部窟室有些还保留了部分造像，共8窟，称之为西崖；东部的窟室均无造像，也无任何石刻碑文，平面多为方形，有少数圆形和椭圆形的窟室，共87窟，称之为东崖。

东部的窟室有单一窟室、一窟双室以及一窟四室等不同类型，有些窟室的入口上方还有采光用的窗口，这些窟室的规模都不大，且各窟室外在崖壁上还可以看到为了互相联系而架设木栈道所留在崖壁上的孔洞痕迹。其中，东崖和中崖洞窟比较密集。开凿较早、规模最大和保存最为完整的是大佛窟，其次是千佛洞和罗汉洞，其大约开凿于唐高宗执政时期。

大佛窟完工于贞观二年（公元628年），是我国现存唐太宗时期规模最大、最为精美的一处洞窟。洞窟南北长18米，东西宽34.5米，高31（24）米，方向为北偏东15度。洞窟内平面近似半圆形，洞窟上部的总体构造为穹隆形。洞窟前有砖木结构的五层楼阁屹立，可以登临眺望。

洞内共有70龛，1001躯造像。洞正中有石雕佛像三尊，阿弥陀佛居中，观音菩萨侍左，大势至菩萨侍右，均为石胎泥塑。阿弥陀佛高27米，宽10米，佛指长2米。

依岩跌坐，肩宽宽厚，披衣袒胸，腰系佩带，造型自然丰满，神情端庄，颇显唐代风格，大佛寺亦因此佛而得名。大佛背光边沿浮雕飞舞在碧空的22躯飞天伎乐，花纹装饰精美，充分表现了初唐的艺术风格。观音菩萨高21.5米，大势至菩萨高21米。

大佛洞内四壁布满佛龛，雕刻大小佛像、菩萨像400余尊，列置最小的仅2厘米。大佛窟规模宏大，造像雄伟，雕刻精致，是古代造像艺术中的一大宝库。从形制来看，大佛窟主大像窟继承了公元4、5世纪佛教自印度传入中国早期时，新疆克孜尔石窟马蹄形平面并主像身后两侧凿出礼拜隧道的做法。

从结构来看，大佛窟顶部采用了敦煌莫高窟中最为流行的木构房屋的横向人字披形式。从雕刻手法来看，大佛身后的背光雕刻，显示出北朝的佛教艺术传统。罗汉洞位于大佛窟西侧，大约开凿于唐高宗时期，共有4个石窟，规模较小。第三窟内壁有浮雕经变故事60余幅，并保存有唐宋以来游人题刻甚多。

其余三窟内，均有石雕和菩萨造像，数量不等，造型与大佛窟相似，雕工细致。千佛洞位于罗汉洞东侧10号窟，大约开凿于唐高宗时期，外观是一明两暗的厅堂，内为石窟。窟内均有佛和菩萨造像，四周墙壁遍布浮雕，共计300余幅，刻画的题材为人物和经变故事，惟妙惟肖。

千佛洞有十几处佛龛均包含着明确的铭文发愿题记，大部分是在武则天执政时期雕造出来的。这个时期的造像都具有健美的身材、婀娜多姿的体态，体现了大唐帝国中最为纯正的佛教造像艺术。特别是在千佛洞中还保存了一处与真人等高的一佛二菩萨造像龛，这处等身像龛的佛与菩萨，是集人体的写实美、夸张美于一体的雕刻艺术，也是千佛洞武则天时期雕刻的代表作品，是最为典型的初唐长安风格的造像艺术。

丈八佛窟又称应福寺，是大佛寺年代最早的佛窟，位于大佛窟以西200米处，沿山开凿的9孔小石窟散布在崖面上。

窟内有依山雕刻的一佛二菩萨站立像，系典型的石胎泥塑造像，肃穆庄重，俊美优雅，具有明显的北周佛教造像艺术特征，显示该窟开凿于南北朝时期。慈善寺石窟位于宝鸡市麟游县城东6公里处漆水河西南岸的崖面之上。

石窟开凿于隋仁寿年间（公元601至604年），唐高宗永徽四年（公元654年）又进行大规模续建。石窟内佛像丰满圆润，刀法洗练，代表了隋唐时期佛造像的最高艺术水平。

它的建筑和雕刻样式曾是当时建造石窟的范本，因而保存下来的洞窟建筑也十分珍贵。2001年公布为第五批全国重点文物保护单位。陕西宝鸡麟游是隋唐帝王避暑的行宫九成宫所在地，慈善寺是当时皇帝后妃及大臣在麟游避暑时礼佛的宫廷寺院。

唐文宗开成元年（公元836年）慈善寺佛舍遭大水毁坏，后多次修复。

现存石窟分布于高28米的崖壁上，西崖有三大窟，南崖有窟龛多座，两处共有12个洞窟，6座佛龛，47尊造像。西崖第一、二窟平面呈马蹄形，窟内佛像体量较大。

从北向南依次编号1至3号窟，第一窟主佛高5.5米，即为隋代开凿镌造。隋代存在时间短促，所遗佛教文物较少，像慈善寺第一窟主佛这样高大完整、保存到今天的隋皇宫石窟造像，极为珍贵。

南崖上有9个摩崖造像龛，内凿佛像，体量较小，不规则地呈东西向排列，尺寸大小不一，且无统一的布局规划。此外，慈善寺石窟窟壁内外还保留有唐代至明代的题记、刻经5处。

慈善寺石窟属于前殿后佛的平面布局，与彬县大佛寺的形制相同，但规模不如前者。这种窟的形制，佛像较大，常占据窟内主要空间，而礼佛活动主要在窟外大殿中进行。1号窟位于西崖的最北面，平面为马蹄形，窟顶近于穹隆，中部平整。窟室内部空间较大，而在窟口处收缩。窟门上部呈圆拱状，已经崩塌，仅南部顶上拐角处保存一小部分弧度，推测窟门原应是拱形。

窟门高6.72米，宽4.45米，进深5.90米。窟内依西（后）、北、南壁下部凿有倒凹字形坛，西坛高1.5、侧坛高0.9米，西坛向外伸出构成主佛下座。坛上依三壁各雕一结跏跌坐佛像。

2号窟位于慈善寺西崖北部1号窟南侧，北距1号窟9.5米。平面呈马蹄形，两侧壁外弧，窟口内收。窟口残损，原应为圆形拱。

窟高538米、窟门宽3.20米、后壁宽3.76米，窟中部最宽5.30米、进深2米。倚着西壁雕一立佛，左右壁下部各开一大龛，上部各开一小龛。左壁大龛内雕一佛二菩萨像，右壁大龛内雕一佛二弟子。

2号窟主佛南侧下距地面0.96米处，刻有佛经一部，高1.30米、宽0.65米许，楷书，字1.5至2厘米见方，共31行。经中有许多字已漫漶不清，仅可辨识的字就有一千余言，无疑可大大地弥补敦煌残卷之缺。敬福经可以堪称为迄今为止发现的全国现存刻写时间最早，保存字数最多，内容最完整的世俗佛经。

3号窟位于北崖2号窟南侧1.5米处，仅仅开凿了窟口，便废弃了。从现存遗迹上看，窟口略成弧顶横长方形，窟内平面呈长方形。窟高4.9米、窟门宽6.17米、进深1.8米，窟口下沿距地面1.9米。

窟室后壁中部有后代补凿的佛龛造像，虽承唐风，但造型较为呆板，手法拙劣。

慈善寺石窟总面积不大，只有两座庙，但特别可贵的是，这尊大佛因未经后代装修，仍保持了初盛唐时代的雕刻特点，反映了初盛唐雕刻造像的雄浑而又精美细致的高超手法，其所雕佛像，头部稍大，肌肤丰满，神态完美，裙带衣纹流畅，刀法简练有力，时代风格鲜明，是隋唐皇家寺院佛教艺术的精品之作。

富县石泓寺石窟又名川子河石窟，位于延安市富县城西65公里处的直罗镇川子河北岸。

石窟依崖凿石而成，2006年公布为第六批全国重点文物保护单位。

从窟内题记看，此窟始凿于隋大业年间（公元605至618年），唐、宋、金、明、清各代都有雕凿，其大多数开凿于唐、宋时期。解县石泓寺，开凿时代在唐初或更早一些，最突出的是金代开凿的窟，大小雕像三千多尊。

窟前有清代修建民国修复的皇经楼，上刻石泓寺三个大字，寺前现有三开间寺楼1座及12间配房。石泓寺坐东北面西南，选址优越。

石窟南面与石山对峙，中间形成200米宽的东西峡谷，川子河从西向东由窟前流过，这里环境优美，风景宜人，是佛教徒修行的好地方。

石窟依崖而凿，在东西长约70米的山崖上共分布10个洞窟，其中7个有石刻造像，共计小龛65个，石造像3371尊。

石窟平面布置上均采用中央佛坛背屏式，窟室与窟口之间不设甬道，平面呈方形，窟口仿木结构做法，雕出檐柱及阑额。

7座石窟中年代较早的是中间的5号窟，其余依次为3号窟、2号窟和4号窟，最外侧的石窟开凿年代较晚，说明各代凿窟均在已有洞窟的两侧进行，属于分布集中、时代连续的石窟群。

5号窟为隋大业年间（公元605至618年）题记，窟口为长方形，窟内面宽2.5米、深3米、高2.1米，中央佛坛起背屏，供一佛二菩萨，东西壁共有造像34尊。

2号窟、3号窟和4号窟的形制相似，窟门呈长方形，两间雕成门楣抱框，窟内平面为长方形，进深大于面宽，窟顶微微凸起，室中央起佛帐，佛像后部均做背屏直通窟顶，背屏和窟内侧壁上有各种造像。

以上三窟的最早题记分别为2号窟北宋开宝二年（公元969年）、3号窟唐咸通五年（公元860年）、4号窟后周显德元年（公元954年）。

6号窟规模最大，造像众多，窟内有甬道，对进深较大的窟室来讲，甬道起到了稳定结构的作用。窟室平面近方形，面阔10.3米、进深10.65米、高5.35米，中央布置佛坛，有一佛二菩萨二弟子，佛与菩萨均结跏跌坐于莲花座上，二弟子分立两旁。佛坛背屏演变成了四根通顶连地的造像柱，加强了视觉的纵深感。

石柱上雕刻造像1131尊，窟室两壁有造像1947尊，窟顶刻有几何图案花纹。造像铭刻最早的年代是金皇统元年（1141年）。在造像内容和雕刻技法上比唐宋时期更广泛、更活泼。

1号窟和7号窟均凿于明代，窟中表现的是佛、道、儒三教合一的内容，在技法上均逊于其他的几个窟，可以看出至明代，造窟之风衰颓。窟内中央不再设佛坛，而将佛坛放到窟室的后壁，人物造像神形刻板，内容芜杂。

米脂万佛洞原名万福洞，位于米脂县城北8公里处的无定河右岸悬崖之上，是陕北地区洞窟最多，规模最大的石窟群。1982年列为县级文物保护单位。

万佛洞建造年代尚不可考，一般认为建于宋至明初。洞窟选址险要，悬崖顺无定河为南北向，横长约500米许，崖顶平坦，崖层距地面约20米，至崖顶约20至30米。悬崖峭壁，怪石嶙峋，顶层向外突出，其状险峻。

万佛洞倚崖而建，主要有伽蓝护法殿、观音洞、灵宫殿、白衣洞、关王洞、九天圣母洞，现存窟内大部造像被毁。

主窟伽蓝护法殿，平面呈方形，宽8米，进深11.6米，高4.7米，窟顶前部有平棋15格，内有浮雕花草、瑞兽等图案，后半部中心为穹隆顶，中为八卦，外围浮雕一周小佛造像等。

窟正面下凿成长方形石坛基，上有3个须弥座，两侧各有一长方形石坛基。窟中有两根长方形石柱，直达窟顶，南北两面宽约1.30米，东西两面宽约1米，上雕刻小佛及题记，在柱中部岩石断层不宜雕像处，改用彩绘。窟两壁凿刻形态各异、造型多样的小佛，间以题记。

正面与南北两面有浮雕佛像，里面无佛像。南柱正面，有横向排列的浮雕佛像15排，计有佛像130尊，其中上部有大肚佛一尊，下部佛像为盘坐，高约0.2米、底座宽0.1米，小佛像高0.5米、底座宽0.1米。

南柱北面，上部有佛像9排，每排有横向排列的浮雕佛像17尊，计有佛像153尊，下部有佛像7排，每排有横向排列的浮雕佛像11尊，计有佛像77尊。

南柱南面，计有佛像18排，每排有横向排列的浮雕佛像16尊，计有佛像288尊。

北柱正面，有横向排列的浮雕佛像17排，上部1至9排每排13尊，下部10至17排，每排8尊，计有佛像181尊。北柱南面与南柱北面佛像的排数与尊数基本相同，计有佛像230尊。

北柱北面，有横向排列的浮雕佛像16排，每排有佛像11尊、13尊、17尊、12尊不等，计有佛像217尊。

洞窟内的正壁，有横向排列的浮雕佛像12排，每排120尊，每排每尊位置上下交错，计有佛像1440尊。

洞窟内的北壁，有横向排列的浮雕佛像18排，每排有佛像147尊，计有佛像2646尊。

洞窟内的南壁，有横向排列的浮雕佛像19排，每排有佛像150尊，计有佛像2850尊。

— ENGLISH —

Shaanxi is one of the regions with relatively developed grotto development in China, mainly featuring late-period grottoes.

Currently, more than 350 grottoes from the Northern Wei to Ming and Qing dynasties have been discovered, among which the more important grottoes include: 9 from the Northern Dynasties, 32 from Sui, Tang, and Five Dynasties, and 73 from Song, Jin, and Yuan.

In Shaanxi grottoes, there are few large caves and many small caves; few group caves and many single caves; few large-scale statues and many small-scale statues; few Northern Dynasties, Sui, and Tang grottoes and many Song grottoes; few grottoes with continuous carving over long periods and many grottoes of a single era; few grottoes in Guanzhong and southern Shaanxi and many in northern Shaanxi; few with complex subject combinations and many with simple subjects.

Northern Dynasties grottoes include Qinjiahe, Huashiya, Fudi, Muxugou, and Pengcun in Yijun County; Jianhua Temple and Yunshan Pin Temple in Ansai County; Shuanglong Xiangfang in Huangling County; Laojun Temple in Ganquan County; and the Great Buddha Temple in Bin County, with the Northern Wei era accounting for the majority.

During this period, the niches and caves of grottoes were difficult to distinguish, with small scales; the Buddhist niches had very shallow depths, with face widths and heights mostly around 1 m.

Buddhist niches were mostly arched or pointed-arched, with some niches having connecting columns on the sides. The statues were mainly one Buddha and two bodhisattvas, or with flying apsaras and Hu-costume donors.

The Yijun Fudi grotto, carved in the 1st year of Datong, Western Wei (535 AD), in addition to Buddhist statues, also carved Taoist Tianzun (Celestial Venerable) statues, with the donors in the dedication inscriptions calling themselves 'daoshi' (Taoist priests) and 'daomin' (Taoist people), making it the earliest dated Buddha-Taoist combined niche statue discovered to date.

Sui and Tang grottoes are numerous, including the revived Buddhist temple in Lantian, Jinsuoguan in Tongchuan, Jiaozhai in Yijun, Great Buddha Temple and Heiquanyi in Ansai, Shihong Temple and Chuanzhuang in Fuxian, Mengjiahe, Fangjiahe, and Liulaozhuang in Ganquan, Shikong Temple in Wuqi, Shijiahe in Luochuan, Qifo Cave, Kaihua Temple, and Chaoyang Cave in Hancheng, Yuhuang Temple in Jiaxian, and Qianfo Temple in Jingbian.

Song, Jin, and Yuan grottoes are mainly distributed in Yan'an City, northern Shaanxi, which is closely related to the Song dynasty's vigorous promotion of Buddhism and the wars between Song and Western Xia.

In the 1st year of Jianlong, Emperor Taizu of Song (960 AD), just a few months after ascending the throne, Emperor Taizu lifted the Later Zhou decree destroying Buddhism, and Buddhist temples revived.

Especially in Ansai, Ganquan, Zichang, Fuxian, and Yan'an, the grottoes are most dense; not only are there many grottoes, but the techniques are also mature, and the subjects tend to be more secularized, with the Zhongshan grotto in Zichang County, Gezitou grotto in Fuxian County, Shuanglong grotto in Huangling County, and Wanfo Cave grotto in Yan'an City being the most representative.

The Bin County Great Buddha Temple grottoes are located on Qingliang Mountain near Dafosi Village, Shuidongdong Township, on the south bank of the Jing River, 10 km west of Bin County town.

It is the largest grotto group in Shaanxi Province and one of the largest and most exquisite grotto groups from the early Tang and high Tang periods existing in China.

It was designated as the third batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 1988.

The Great Buddha Temple grottoes were first carved during the Northern and Southern Dynasties period, with large-scale carving in the early Tang, basically completed in the 2nd year of Zhenguan (628 AD).

Originally named Yingfu Temple, it was built by Emperor Taizong of Tang to bring blessings to the soldiers who died in the battle against Xue Ju and Xue Rengao.

When Emperor Renzong of Northern Song celebrated the birthday of his adoptive mother Empress Dowager Liu across the empire, it was renamed Qingshou Temple.

Because of its tall and majestic Buddha statues, people commonly called it the Great Buddha Temple from the Jingtai period of Ming and this name has been used ever since.

The Great Buddha Temple grotto group faces north with its back to south, carving caves into the mountain and carving statues from stone, arranged along the east-west direction on steep vertical cliff faces.

Currently, there are 361 large and small caves and niches, including 107 caves and 254 Buddhist niches; there are 19 caves with statues in total, with 1,498 statue bodies, distributed in an orderly manner on a three-dimensional cliff face about 400 m long, divided into five parts: Great Buddha Cave, Thousand-Buddha Cave, Arhat Cave (Buddha Cave), Zhangba Buddha Cave, and Monk Room Caves.

The grotto group centers on the Great Buddha Cave as the core of the entire temple and can be divided into three major parts: central, western, and eastern.

The central part is the main part of the entire grotto group, including the Great Buddha Cave and the adjacent Thousand-Buddha Cave and Arhat Cave on both sides, totaling 12 caves, called the 'Central Cliff,' i.e., the Mirror Platform; the western cave chambers still retain some statues, totaling 8 caves, called the 'West Cliff'; the eastern cave chambers have no statues and no stone inscriptions, mostly square in plane, with a few circular and oval cave chambers, totaling 87 caves, called the 'East Cliff.' The eastern cave chambers include different types such as single cave chambers, one-cave double-chamber, and one-cave four-chamber; some cave chambers have lighting windows above the entrances; these cave chambers are not large in scale, and on the cliff outside each cave chamber, traces of holes left by wooden walkways erected for mutual connection can still be seen.

Among them, the East Cliff and Central Cliff caves are relatively dense. The earliest carved, largest-scale, and best-preserved cave is the Great Buddha Cave, followed by the Thousand-Buddha Cave and Arhat Cave, which were approximately carved during the reign of Emperor Gaozong of Tang.

The Great Buddha Cave was completed in the 2nd year of Zhenguan (628 AD), making it the largest and most exquisite cave from the reign of Emperor Taizong of Tang existing in China. The cave is 18 m long north-south, 34.5 m wide east-west, and 31 (24) m high, oriented 15 degrees east of north.

The interior plane of the cave is approximately semicircular, and the overall structure of the upper part of the cave is dome-shaped. In front of the cave, a five-story brick-wood structure pavilion stands, allowing climbing and viewing. Inside the cave, there are 70 niches and 1,001 statue bodies.

In the center of the cave, there are three stone-carved Buddha statues: Amitabha Buddha in the center, Guanyin Bodhisattva on the left, and Mahasthamaprapta Bodhisattva on the right, all of stone-clay construction. Amitabha Buddha is 27 m high and 10 m wide, with a Buddha finger 2 m long.

Sitting cross-legged against the rock, with broad shoulders, robes draping and chest exposed, a waist sash, a naturally plump form, and a dignified expression, quite showing the Tang style; the Great Buddha Temple is also named after this Buddha.

On the edge of the Great Buddha's halo, 22 flying apsara musicians are relief-carved dancing in the blue sky, with exquisitely decorated patterns, fully expressing the artistic style of the early Tang. Guanyin Bodhisattva is 21.5 m high, and Mahasthamaprapta Bodhisattva is 21 m high.

The four walls inside the Great Buddha Cave are covered with Buddhist niches, carving more than 400 large and small Buddha and bodhisattva statues, with the smallest being only 2 cm.

The Great Buddha Cave is grand in scale, with majestic statues and exquisite carvings, making it a great treasure house of ancient statue art.

In terms of form, the Great Buddha Cave's main large statue cave inherits the practice of the Kizil grottoes in Xinjiang from the 4th and 5th centuries, when Buddhism was first introduced to China from India—horse-shoe-shaped plane with worship tunnels carved on both sides behind the main statue.

In terms of structure, the top of the Great Buddha Cave adopts the most popular horizontal herringbone roof form of wooden buildings in the Mogao grottoes at Dunhuang.

In terms of carving techniques, the halo carving behind the Great Buddha shows the Buddhist art tradition of the Northern Dynasties.

The Arhat Cave is located west of the Great Buddha Cave, approximately carved during the reign of Emperor Gaozong of Tang, with a total of 4 grottoes, small in scale.

The inner wall of the third cave has more than 60 relief-carved sutra illustration stories, and preserves many inscriptions by visitors since the Tang and Song dynasties.

In the remaining three caves, there are stone-carved and bodhisattva statues of varying quantities, with forms similar to the Great Buddha Cave and exquisite carving. The Thousand-Buddha Cave is located in Cave No.

10 east of the Arhat Cave, approximately carved during the reign of Emperor Gaozong of Tang; its exterior is a hall with one bright and two dark bays, with grottoes inside.

Inside the cave, there are Buddha and bodhisattva statues, and the surrounding walls are covered with relief carvings, totaling more than 300, depicting subjects of figures and sutra illustration stories, vivid and lifelike.

The Thousand-Buddha Cave has more than ten Buddhist niches all containing clear dedication inscriptions, most carved during the reign of Wu Zetian. The statues from this period all have vigorous figures and graceful postures, reflecting the purest Buddhist statue art of the Great Tang Empire.

Especially in the Thousand-Buddha Cave, a one-Buddha-two-bodhisattva statue niche at human height is preserved; the Buddha and bodhisattvas in this life-size niche are a carving art integrating realistic beauty and exaggerated beauty of the human body, and are also representative works carved during the Wu Zetian period in the Thousand-Buddha Cave, being the most typical early Tang Chang'an-style statue art.

The Zhangba Buddha Cave, also called Yingfu Temple, is the earliest Buddhist cave at the Great Buddha Temple, located 200 m west of the Great Buddha Cave, with 9 small grottoes carved along the mountain scattered on the cliff face.

Inside the cave, there are one Buddha and two bodhisattva standing statues carved against the mountain, typical stone-clay statues, solemn and dignified, handsome and elegant, with obvious Northern Zhou Buddhist statue art characteristics, indicating that this cave was carved during the Northern and Southern Dynasties period.

The Cishan Temple grottoes are located on the cliff face on the southwest bank of the Qi River, 6 km east of Linyou County town, Baoji City.

The grottoes were carved during the Renshou period of Sui (601-604 AD), and large-scale additional construction was carried out in the 4th year of Yonghui, Emperor Gaozong of Tang (654 AD).

The Buddha statues inside the grottoes are plump and rounded, with refined carving techniques, representing the highest artistic level of Buddha statues in the Sui-Tang period.

Its architectural and carving styles were once the models for building grottoes at that time, so the preserved cave architecture is also very precious. It was designated as the fifth batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 2001.

Linyou in Baoji, Shaanxi, is the location of the Jiucheng Palace, the summer palace of Sui-Tang emperors; Cishan Temple was the court temple where emperors, empresses, and ministers worshipped Buddha during their summer stays in Linyou.

In the 1st year of Kaicheng, Emperor Wenzong of Tang (836 AD), the Cishan Temple Buddhist hall was destroyed by flood, and was repaired multiple times afterwards.

The existing grottoes are distributed on a cliff 28 m high, with three large caves on the west cliff and many cave niches on the south cliff; the two places have a total of 12 caves, 6 Buddhist niches, and 47 statues.

The first and second caves on the west cliff have horse-shoe-shaped planes, with large Buddha statues inside. Numbered 1 to 3 from north to south, the main Buddha of Cave 1 is 5.5 m high, carved during the Sui dynasty.

The Sui dynasty existed for a short time, leaving few Buddhist cultural relics; a Sui imperial palace grotto statue as tall, complete, and preserved to today as the main Buddha of Cave 1 at Cishan Temple is extremely precious.

On the south cliff, there are 9 cliff statue niches with Buddha statues carved inside, small in scale, irregularly arranged east-west, with varying sizes and no unified layout plan.

In addition, inside and outside the cave walls of Cishan Temple grottoes, 5 inscriptions and carved scriptures from the Tang to Ming dynasties are preserved.

The Cishan Temple grottoes belong to the 'front hall, rear Buddha' plane layout, same as the form of the Great Buddha Temple in Bin County, but smaller in scale.

In this cave form, the Buddha statue is large, often occupying the main space inside the cave, while Buddhist worship activities mainly take place in the hall outside the cave.

Cave 1 is located at the northernmost part of the west cliff, with a horse-shoe-shaped plane; the cave top is nearly dome-shaped, flat in the middle. The interior space of the cave chamber is large, while contracting at the cave entrance.

The upper part of the cave door is round-arched, having collapsed, with only a small part of the arc preserved at the top corner on the south side; it is推测 that the cave door should originally have been arched. The cave door is 6.72 m high, 4.45 m wide, and 5.90 m deep.

Inside the cave, along the lower parts of the west (rear), north, and south walls, an inverted-concave-shaped altar is carved; the west altar is 1.5 m high and the side altars are 0.9 m high, with the west altar extending outward to form the main Buddha's lower seat.
