On the altar, along each of the three walls, one cross-legged sitting Buddha statue is carved. Cave 2 is located south of Cave 1 on the northern part of the west cliff, 9.5 m north of Cave 1.

Its plane is horse-shoe-shaped, with both side walls curving outward and the cave entrance contracting inward. The cave entrance is damaged, originally should have been a round arch.

The cave is 5.38 m high, the cave door is 3.20 m wide, the rear wall is 3.76 m wide, the widest part in the middle of the cave is 5.30 m, and the depth is 2 m.

A standing Buddha is carved against the west wall; the lower parts of the left and right walls each have a large niche, and the upper parts each have a small niche.

Inside the large niche on the left wall, one Buddha and two bodhisattva statues are carved; inside the large niche on the right wall, one Buddha and two disciples are carved.

On the south side of the main Buddha in Cave 2, 0.96 m above the ground, a Buddhist scripture is carved, 1.30 m high and about 0.65 m wide, in regular script, with characters 1.5 to 2 cm square, totaling 31 lines.

Many characters in the scripture have become blurred and illegible, with only more than a thousand characters discernible, which can undoubtedly greatly弥补 the gaps in the Dunhuang fragmentary scrolls.

The Jingfu Sutra can be called the earliest-dated, most-character-preserving, and most-complete secular Buddhist scripture existing in the country discovered to date. Cave 3 is located 1.5 m south of Cave 2 on the north cliff; only the cave entrance was carved before it was abandoned.

From the existing remains, the cave entrance is slightly arc-topped and horizontally rectangular, and the interior plane of the cave is rectangular. The cave is 4.9 m high, the cave door is 6.17 m wide, the depth is 1.8 m, and the lower edge of the cave entrance is 1.9 m above the ground.

In the middle of the rear wall of the cave chamber, there is a later-carved Buddhist niche statue, although following the Tang style, the form is relatively stiff and the technique is crude.

The total area of Cishan Temple grottoes is not large, with only two temples, but what is particularly valuable is that this Great Buddha, because it has not been renovated by later generations, still maintains the carving characteristics of the early and high Tang periods, reflecting the vigorous and exquisite techniques of early and high Tang carved statues; the carved Buddha statues have slightly larger heads, plump skin, perfect expressions, flowing robe and sash patterns, and powerful and concise carving techniques, with a distinct era style, making them fine works of Buddhist art of Sui-Tang royal temples.

The Fuxian Shihong Temple grottoes, also called Chuanzi River grottoes, are located on the north bank of the Chuanzi River, Zhiluo Town, 65 km west of Fuxian County town, Yan'an City.

The grottoes are carved into the cliff, and were designated as the sixth batch of National Key Cultural Heritage Sites in 2006.

From the inscriptions inside the caves, these grottoes were first carved during the Daye period of Sui (605-618 AD), with carvings in the Tang, Song, Jin, Ming, and Qing dynasties, most carved during the Tang and Song periods.

The Shihong Temple in Xie County, its carving era was in the early Tang or earlier, with the most prominent being the caves carved in the Jin dynasty, with more than three thousand large and small statues.

In front of the caves, there is a Huangjing Tower built in the Qing dynasty and repaired in the Republic period, with the three characters 'Shihong Temple' carved on it; in front of the temple, there is currently a three-bay temple tower and 12 side rooms.

Shihong Temple faces southwest with its back to northeast, with an excellent site selection.

The south side of the grottoes faces a stone mountain, forming an east-west gorge 200 m wide in the middle, with the Chuanzi River flowing from west to east in front of the caves; the environment here is beautiful and the scenery pleasant, making it a good place for Buddhist practitioners.

The grottoes are carved into the cliff, with a total of 10 caves distributed on a cliff about 70 m long east-west, among which 7 have stone-carved statues, totaling 65 small niches and 3,371 stone statues.

The plane layout of the grottoes all adopts the central Buddhist altar with back screen style, with no corridor between the cave chamber and the cave entrance; the plane is square, and the cave entrance adopts imitation wooden structure techniques, carving out eave columns and architraves.

Among the 7 grottoes, the earliest is Cave 5 in the middle, followed by Cave 3, Cave 2, and Cave 4; the outermost grottoes were carved later, indicating that each dynasty carved caves on both sides of existing caves, belonging to a grotto group with concentrated distribution and continuous eras.

Cave 5 has an inscription from the Daye period of Sui (605-618 AD); the cave entrance is rectangular, with an interior width of 2.5 m, depth of 3 m, and height of 2.1 m; a central Buddhist altar with back screen enshrines one Buddha and two bodhisattvas, with a total of 34 statues on the east and west walls.

Caves 2, 3, and 4 have similar forms; the cave doors are rectangular, with two bays carved into door lintel and door frame; the interior plane is rectangular, with depth greater than width, the cave top slightly raised, a Buddhist canopy in the center of the chamber, and the back of the Buddha statues all made into back screens直通 the cave top; various statues are on the back screens and the inner side walls of the cave.

The earliest inscriptions of the above three caves are respectively: Cave 2, the 2nd year of Kaibao, Northern Song (969 AD); Cave 3, the 5th year of Xiantong, Tang (860 AD); Cave 4, the 1st year of Xiande, Later Zhou (954 AD).

Cave 6 is the largest in scale with numerous statues; there is a corridor inside the cave, which for a cave chamber with large depth plays a role in structural stability.

The cave chamber plane is nearly square, with a width of 10.3 m, depth of 10.65 m, and height of 5.35 m; a Buddhist altar is arranged in the center, with one Buddha, two bodhisattvas, and two disciples; the Buddha and bodhisattvas all sit cross-legged on lotus seats, with the two disciples standing on both sides.

The Buddhist altar back screen evolved into four statue columns connecting the top and ground, enhancing the visual sense of depth. 1,131 statues are carved on the stone columns, 1,947 statues on the two walls of the cave chamber, and geometric pattern decorations on the cave top.

The earliest date of statue inscriptions is the 1st year of Huangtong, Jin (1141 AD). In terms of statue content and carving techniques, it is more extensive and lively than in the Tang and Song periods.

Caves 1 and 7 were both carved in the Ming dynasty; the caves express the content of the integration of Buddhism, Taoism, and Confucianism, and in terms of techniques are inferior to the other caves, showing that by the Ming dynasty, the trend of cave carving had declined.

The center of the cave no longer has a Buddhist altar, but the Buddhist altar is placed at the rear wall of the cave chamber; the figure statues are stiff in form and miscellaneous in content.

The Mizhi Wanfo Cave, originally named Wanfu Cave, is located on the cliff on the right bank of the Wuding River, 8 km north of Mizhi County town. It is the grotto group with the most caves and largest scale in northern Shaanxi.

It was listed as a county-level cultural heritage protection unit in 1982. The construction date of Wanfo Cave is still unknown, generally believed to have been built from the Song to early Ming.

The cave site is strategically located on a precipice; the cliff runs north-south along the Wuding River, about 500 m long horizontally, with a flat cliff top; the cliff layer is about 20 m above the ground and about 20 to 30 m to the cliff top.

With steep cliffs and jagged rocks, the top layer protrudes outward, in a precipitous form.

Wanfo Cave is built against the cliff, mainly including the Qielan Hufa Hall, Guanyin Cave, Lingguan Hall, Baiyi Cave, Guanwang Cave, and Jiutian Shengmu Cave; most statues in the existing caves have been destroyed.

The main cave, Qielan Hufa Hall, has a square plane, 8 m wide, 11.6 m deep, and 4.7 m high; the front part of the cave top has 15 coffered ceiling squares, with relief-carved patterns of flowers, plants, and auspicious beasts inside; the center of the rear half is a dome top, with the Eight Trigrams in the center and a ring of small Buddha statues relief-carved around the outside.

Below the front face of the cave, a rectangular stone altar base is carved, with 3 xumizuo (Sumeru pedestals) on it, and a rectangular stone altar base on each side.

There are two rectangular stone columns in the cave, reaching the cave top, about 1.30 m wide on the north and south faces and about 1 m wide on the east and west faces, with small Buddhas and inscriptions carved on them; at the rock fault in the middle of the columns where statues are not suitable, colored paintings are used instead.

The two walls of the cave are carved with small Buddhas of various forms and shapes, interspersed with inscriptions. The front face and north and south faces have relief Buddha statues, while the inner face has no Buddha statues.

On the front face of the south column, there are 15 rows of horizontally arranged relief Buddha statues, totaling 130 Buddha statues, among which the upper part has one Big-belly Buddha statue, and the lower Buddha statues are cross-legged sitting, about 0.2 m high with a 0.1 m wide base, while the small Buddha statues are 0.5 m high with a 0.1 m wide base.

On the north face of the south column, the upper part has 9 rows of Buddha statues, each row with 17 horizontally arranged relief Buddha statues, totaling 153 Buddha statues; the lower part has 7 rows of Buddha statues, each row with 11 horizontally arranged relief Buddha statues, totaling 77 Buddha statues.

On the south face of the south column, there are 18 rows of Buddha statues, each row with 16 horizontally arranged relief Buddha statues, totaling 288 Buddha statues.

On the front face of the north column, there are 17 rows of horizontally arranged relief Buddha statues, with rows 1 to 9 having 13 statues each and rows 10 to 17 having 8 statues each, totaling 181 Buddha statues.

The south face of the north column has basically the same number of rows and statues as the north face of the south column, totaling 230 Buddha statues.

On the north face of the north column, there are 16 rows of horizontally arranged relief Buddha statues, with each row having 11, 13, 17, or 12 statues不等, totaling 217 Buddha statues.

On the front wall inside the cave, there are 12 rows of horizontally arranged relief Buddha statues, with 120 statues per row, and each statue position staggered up and down per row, totaling 1,440 Buddha statues.

On the north wall inside the cave, there are 18 rows of horizontally arranged relief Buddha statues, with 147 statues per row, totaling 2,646 Buddha statues.

On the south wall inside the cave, there are 19 rows of horizontally arranged relief Buddha statues, with 150 statues per row, totaling 2,850 Buddha statues.

关键特征 / Key Features

  • 陕西是我国石窟发展比较发达的地区之一
  • 以晚期石窟为主
  • 目前共发现北魏至明、清时期石窟350余处
  • 其中比较重要的石窟
  • 北朝9处
  • 隋、唐、五代32处
  • 宋、金、元73处
  • 陕西石窟中大型窟少
  • 小型窟多
  • 群体窟少
  • 单体窟多
  • 大体量造像少
  • 小体量造像多
  • 北朝、隋、唐石窟少
  • 宋代石窟较多
